# Abundance Engineering
How the Giving Economy Moves the World Left of Zero

The science of designing systems that give more than they take

What if the world isn’t limited by scarcity—but by how we design our systems?

In *Abundance Engineering*, Josh Cliffords introduces a new framework for understanding how value actually flows through economies—and how it can be redesigned to give more than it takes. At the center of the book is the concept of the **economic number line**, a model that maps systems from traditional scarcity-based markets to a new domain “left of zero,” where participation itself generates value. In this space, products don’t just become free—they can become **negatively priced**, creating benefits for users, businesses, and society at the same time.

Building on this foundation, the book introduces key ideas such as **honest data**—a clearer signal of real human preference once price distortion is removed—and shows how systems can be redesigned so that increasing efficiency naturally expands access, reduces waste, and compounds value across entire networks. It also explores a deeper shift: that the physical world itself—our infrastructure, materials, and technologies—can be engineered to either drain value or generate it over time. When systems are designed to be regenerative by default, the pressure that defines scarcity begins to disappear. Together, these ideas form a new discipline: **Abundance Engineering**—the science of designing systems that give more than they take. This is not a theory of what might happen someday. It is a framework for what is already beginning—and a blueprint for how the world moves beyond scarcity.

## Abstract

The cost of producing essential goods and services has been approaching zero for decades. In some sectors — information, software, renewable electricity after payback, advertising-subsidized consumer products — that cost has already crossed zero, meaning the system returns more value to participants than they contribute. Economics has not developed a framework for what happens to markets when price is no longer the primary organizing mechanism. This book is that framework.

*Abundance Engineering* introduces the **economic number line**: a map of value flows that extends from traditional scarcity economics on the right side of zero, through the Free Economy at zero, into the Giving Economy, regenerative systems, and what the book calls Everyone's Economy — the furthest point, where abundance is produced not by external funding but by the system's own efficiency. The book's central claim is that this leftward migration is already underway, that it can be accelerated through deliberate design, and that the resulting systems can be evaluated, measured, and scaled.

The book integrates insights from economics, thermodynamics, infrastructure design, data systems, and the physics of computation. It provides frameworks, applicable across domains, for identifying when a system is extractive versus regenerative, where the constraints on crossing zero actually sit, and what architectural changes move systems left. These frameworks cover value chains, data quality, economic transition, regenerative energy, and the physical constraints on intelligence, among others. Each framework is a test a reader can apply to the systems they build or evaluate.

The book does not argue that scarcity will disappear or that markets will become utopian. Its claim is more specific: that in well-defined domains — energy, food, information, infrastructure, intelligence — systems can be engineered to produce more value than they consume, fund their own replacement and expansion, and return surplus to the participants who enable them. When such systems are integrated — as in the Vertical Engines described in the book's final chapters — they begin to compound, and the infrastructure of a civilization starts to behave less like a cost center and more like a productive ecosystem.

Much of the theoretical work in these pages is grounded in real-world experiments, most notably the development of FreeWater — a bottled water distributed at no cost to the consumer, funded by advertising, with a charitable contribution embedded in every unit. The operational mechanics of building such products were first outlined in the author's prior book, *How to Make a Free Product Company.* The present work extends those foundations into a broader theoretical architecture.

Together, the two books define a new discipline: **Abundance Engineering** — the deliberate design of systems that give more than they take. The first book is the playbook. The second is the map.

The central argument is not whether abundance is possible, but whether it can be engineered. The evidence presented here is that it can be — and that the design work has already begun.

## Introduction: Crossing the Line Beyond Zero

In the summer of 2021, a pallet of bottled water rolled through a distribution center in Austin, Texas. The water was free. The company that made it — my company — still earned a profit. Part of that profit went to clean-water projects in places without safe drinking water. So the person who took a bottle paid nothing, got something they needed, and — through the ad printed on the label — helped fund clean water on the other side of the world, just by drinking.

By every rule of traditional economics, none of that should be possible. A company can't give its product away and survive. A customer can't get something valuable for free and create value for others at the same time. And yet the pallet moved. The ads paid for everything. The donations arrived. The company stayed in business. The math worked.

That pallet proved something much bigger than a clever business model: the number line of economics runs further left than anyone has bothered to map.

Picture that number line. To the right of zero sit the products that cost money — which is almost the entire economy. At zero sit the free ones. And to the left of zero is a region economics doesn't even have words for: products that hand back more value than they ask of you. A free bottle of water with a donation built in. A free search engine that gives away all its profit — Ecosia has funded more than 200 million trees this way. Solar panels that pay themselves off, keep producing value for decades, and can use part of that surplus to build the next system. Every one of these already exists. Each one, in its own corner of the economy, has crossed zero.

This book is about that crossing.

For most of human history, every economic system was built on a single assumption: there is not enough to go around. Capitalism, socialism, mercantilism, feudalism, even the sharing economy — different politics, same foundation. All of them assume that goods must cost something, and that price is the main tool connecting supply to demand. And for most of history, that assumption was simply true. Making things was expensive. Moving things was expensive. Organizing people was expensive.

That reality has changed. Renewable energy, once installed, produces power at almost no extra cost. Information can be copied for free. Automation keeps cutting the cost of coordination. And whole new ways of funding a product — advertising, subscriptions, sponsorship, data — now exist that didn't for most of economic history. The cost of producing life's essentials has been falling for decades. In some categories, it has already crossed zero.

What's missing isn't technology. What's missing is a framework — a way to understand what happens to an economy when price stops being the thing that organizes it.

That framework is this book.

One thing to get straight early, because the examples can hide it. Most of the worked examples ahead start with advertising — a bottle whose printed surface pays for the water inside. It would be easy to conclude that this whole framework is just an advertising model. It isn't. Advertising is the finishing layer: real money, useful money, but limited money. It funds the last stretch past zero and the donation on top, and there is only so much of it in the world. The engine underneath is physical: energy that arrives free every day, captured by equipment that outlasts its own cost, with the surplus reinvested cycle after cycle. That engine — the regenerative energy you'll meet in Chapter 14 — asks nothing of any ad market, and it lowers the cost of everything built on top of it. Efficiency is what carries a product toward zero. Advertising is what carries it the final step past. Chapter 3 puts numbers on that division, and they are not what most readers expect: on an ordinary essential, efficiency covers roughly seven-eighths of the distance, and the final step past is a much shorter one than the sticker price suggests. And the two layers have different ceilings: anything funded by advertising is limited by the size of the advertising market, while the engine is limited only by how fast we can build it — and how consistently its surplus gets reinvested. Keep those two layers separate in your head, and everything that follows will read the way it was meant to.

Here's where we're going. The chapters ahead map the number line, explain the mechanisms that move products leftward along it, and describe the infrastructure that makes each crossing possible — all the way to the final stage, what I call Everyone's Economy, where abundance no longer needs anyone to fund it because the system produces enough on its own. Along the way you'll pick up a set of tools you can point at any business or industry: how to tell when a value chain can cross zero, when energy becomes regenerative, when data becomes honest, and when infrastructure stops costing money and starts making it.

None of it is armchair theory. It's built on real experiments — FreeWater and the projects that followed it — and on patterns you can already watch happening: the falling cost of renewable energy, the collapse of one scarcity-based industry after another over the past twenty years, and the handful of economies and companies that have already crossed zero in specific categories.

**This is not a book about what should happen. It's a book about what is already happening, what can be sped up, and what it looks like when it's finished.**

The number line runs further left than economics has mapped. This book is the map.

# Part 1: THE OLD SYSTEMS & THE SHIFT BEYOND ZERO

## Chapter 1: Scarcity vs. Abundance

In 1768, Encyclopedia Britannica published its first edition. For 244 years it was one of the most profitable reference works in the English language — a thirty-two-volume set that sold for about \$1,500 and sat on the shelves of serious households, libraries, and schools around the world. In 2012, it printed its last edition. The book that had outlasted empires, revolutions, and two world wars was killed by a free website written by volunteers.

That website, Wikipedia, now holds many times more content than Britannica ever did. No one is paid to write it. No one pays to read it. And it didn't just compete with the paid encyclopedia — it ended it.

But notice where Wikipedia stopped. It reached free, and it parked there. It is the most successful free product in history, and in twenty-five years it has never returned a cent to the people who built it. No payment, no ownership, no stake anyone could ever cash in. It went exactly as far as free and no further. Hold onto that, because it's the thing this whole book turns on. The story everyone tells about Wikipedia is that free is powerful. The story no one tells is that free is where it stopped.

This is not a story about the internet. It's a story about what happens when something scarce stops being scarce — and about a doorway, standing open at zero, that almost no one has walked through.

For all of history, the rules of economic life took one thing for granted: there is not enough to go around. Almost everything humans have built — markets, prices, property, the reflex to charge — is downstream of that single assumption. What follows is the story of that assumption quietly breaking.

### The free soil that built private fortunes

Free is older and stranger than the internet makes it look, and the most revealing examples are hiding underneath everything else.

Think about the signal coming out of the phone in your pocket. The Global Positioning System was built by the United States government at enormous public cost — and then, in a decision almost no one remembers being made, it was opened to the entire planet for free. No license. No meter. No bill. On top of that free signal grew an economy that didn't exist before it: ride-share, precision farming, package delivery, the blue dot that tells billions of phones where they are. By one widely cited estimate, GPS has generated more than a trillion dollars in economic value. The same goes for free government weather data, which a multibillion-dollar forecasting industry is built on — the weather apps on your phone, the models that route airlines and price crop insurance. In both cases, the public built the instrument and gave the signal away. Private companies built the fortune on top of it, dollar by dollar, on ground they were handed for nothing.

This is not a charity story. It's the clearest proof of a pattern: when something foundational becomes free, it doesn't destroy value — it becomes the ground a new economy grows on. And once again, the free part stopped at the floor. The signal was free; everything built on it was sold. The free layer held there like a foundation, while the businesses standing on it lived entirely on the right side of zero, charging the way businesses always have.

### Where the entrepreneurs stopped

When entrepreneurs reached free on their own, they stopped at the same line.

YouTube is the cleanest case. In 2005, watching what you wanted, when you wanted, meant paying for cable and taking whatever the network scheduled. YouTube made it free — and built something bigger than a video site on top of it: an entire creator economy, where people earn real livings from a platform that charges viewers nothing, funded by advertising that flows back to the people making the work. That is the Free Economy running at planetary scale: access is free, participation creates value, and value circulates back. It's a real achievement, and an entrepreneurial one. No government built YouTube.

And it, too, parked at free. YouTube is free, but it has never been better than free. It doesn't pay you to watch. Yes, it pays a small slice of creators at the top — but from the ordinary viewer it takes something real back: your attention, sold to the highest bidder. Free search did the same thing. It hit zero in the 1990s and has sat there for a quarter-century. Free social media, free email, free maps — an entire digital economy sprinted to the line marked zero, fifteen and twenty years ago, and then just stood on it. Competing on features. Never once stepping across.

### Two reasons free has always existed — and the one no one used

Every product sits somewhere on a single line. To the right of zero, it's for sale — you pay. At zero, it's free. But free comes in two kinds: paid for from outside the business, or funded by advertising that quietly extracts value from you — your attention, your data — while you use it. And past zero, a product can be better than free — returning more value than it asks for, funded not from outside but by the business's own surplus. Free is the midpoint almost everyone stopped at.

Almost every free thing in this chapter stopped there for one of two reasons. The gap between those two reasons is the opening this book was written to fill.

Some things are free because an actor outside the market removed the profit motive. A government gives away the GPS signal or the weather feed. A volunteer community writes the encyclopedia and asks for nothing back. There's no business model because, on purpose, there's no business. Free, here, is what's left when someone takes the profit out.

People have understood the power of giving for a very long time — far longer than they've understood how to fund it. Two thousand years ago, Caesar handed out free grain to the citizens of Rome, and he knew exactly what it bought him: not just quiet, but the feeling that the people owed him something back. That instinct was right, and it never went away. A gift creates a debt, and the debt can be worth more than the gift. What Caesar lacked was an engine. His grain was paid for by taxes and conquest — a cost the treasury absorbed to keep a crowd content. The giving produced nothing, so it could only ever be a handout dressed up as generosity. For two thousand years, that was the ceiling: you could give to win goodwill, but the giving drained you. Which meant only the powerful could afford to do it, and only for as long as the conquest lasted.

Other things are free because an entrepreneur reached zero and got someone else to pay. YouTube, free search, free-to-play games — a business, competing to win, drove its price to nothing and covered the gap with advertising. Here there is very much a business. Free is a strategy, not an absence.

Now look at what none of them did. The governments stopped at free because charity has no reason to go further. Caesar stopped at free because giving without an engine could only ever be a cost. And the entrepreneurs stopped at free because nobody had shown them there was anywhere further to go. Not one of them used the profit motive — the most powerful engine humans have ever built — to travel past free, into territory where a product isn't just free but negatively priced: giving back more value than it asks for, whether that value goes to the user or out to a cause. The company gives more than it takes and still makes money. Free was where the charities began, where Caesar's treasury ran dry, and where the entrepreneurs stopped. The space on the far side of it is the space almost no one has entered.

Almost no one. A search engine called Ecosia gives 100% of its profit away to plant trees and has funded more than 200 million of them — better than free, with the value flowing out to the world. And in 2021, a pallet of bottled water moved through a distribution center in Texas. It was free to whoever took it, and it carried a donation in every bottle — better than free, with value flowing to both the person and a cause. A tiny handful of operators have only just begun to cross. They looked at zero and saw a doorway instead of a wall.

This book is about that doorway: what lies past it, why the engine of profit can be pointed through it instead of stopped at it, and what an economy looks like when businesses compete not over who charges the least, but over who gives the most.

### The door no one walked through

For most of human history, scarcity wasn't an assumption — it was physics. Farmers grew only what the weather allowed; a drought meant famine. Factories built only what workers could physically assemble. Every good demanded labor, materials, and time, and all three were limited. Under those conditions, competition was unavoidable, and for the resources that mattered most, economics was zero-sum: if one person gained more land, food, or wealth, someone else had less. Markets, property, contracts, law — the entire machinery of modern economic life evolved to manage a world where there was not enough to go around.

Then technology changed the math. One farmer learned to feed thousands. A factory could stamp out goods at a scale no craftsman could imagine. And then computers pushed the change somewhere genuinely new: the cost of copying collapsed. A piece of software could be reproduced a million times for nothing. A message could reach the far side of the planet for nothing. Notice the precise thing that went to zero. Not the cost of making the first copy — that's as real as it ever was. The cost of making the next one. Creation still costs. Duplication became free. That distinction looks small, and it's the hinge everything else hangs on.

Because here's what that collapse did: it opened a door. For the first time, you could give a thing to one more person at no cost to yourself — and then to a billion more, still at no cost. The wall that scarcity had built around every product — the one that forced you to charge just to survive — simply fell away, in one industry after another. The door stood open. And as the opening pages of this chapter showed, people walked right up to it. Governments, volunteers, the entire digital economy. They reached free, and they stopped.

That's the real puzzle. Not that free is powerful — everyone knows free is powerful. The puzzle is the stopping. Markets are relentless about profit. Wherever there's an edge, competition finds it, takes it, and forces everyone else to follow or fall behind. If there were obvious money on the far side of that door, the pressure to cross would have been overwhelming, and someone would have crossed decades ago. So the question is not why abundance feels uncomfortable. The question is why an open door, with competitors crowded right up against it, stayed shut.

Two reasons — and they hold each other in place.

The first: nothing forced anyone across. Once you reach free, your competitor is free too. Free versus free is not a price war, because price has nowhere left to fall. The fighting moves to features, interface, catalog size — anything except the number on the tag, which is stuck at zero for everyone. So free became a place you could rest. No competitor was undercutting from below zero. No rival was paying customers more than you paid them. Nothing said go past free or die. Free wasn't a cliff edge. It was a comfortable floor, and everyone stood on it together, because no one was being pushed off.

The second: no one had the move in their hands to begin with. Every founder, every MBA, every investor was trained on one model — price covers cost, the gap is margin, the business keeps the margin. Value and price are treated as the same thing: what something is worth is what you can charge for it. Run "pay the customer to take something that's already free" through that model, and it doesn't return a business. It returns a rounding error, a charity, or a fraud. You can't execute a play you've never been shown exists — and no business school has ever drawn this one on the board.

Now watch the two reasons lock together. There was no competitive pressure, so no one was ever forced to question the model every business runs on — price equals value, and you survive by charging. And that model is the second half of the lock, because inside it, the move past free isn't an undiscovered strategy waiting to be found. It's nonsense — something that reads as charity or fraud the instant anyone proposes it. So the people who might have found the move couldn't see it. And because no one found it, no one was ever undercut by it, and no pressure ever built to make anyone look harder. The missing incentive kept people from searching; the blinding assumption hid the thing from anyone who searched anyway. Each absence guarantees the other — because a missing incentive and a blinding assumption are exactly the two things that keep a discovery buried. It's a trap that holds itself closed. And traps like that don't wear out slowly. They hold, and hold, and hold — until someone stops trusting the model long enough to look past it.

You can see the cost of not breaking it in how hard the old world fights when free merely arrives — never mind beyond free. Britannica didn't go quietly; it spent years insisting its paid product was simply better before it finally stopped printing in 2012. Cable companies lobbied and sued against the open web. Proprietary software fought open source for a decade. And the standalone GPS-device makers watched the free map in every pocket erase them in a few years. In every case, the incumbent treated free as an attack to survive, because its whole model was built on the wall — and not one of them imagined that the winning answer might sit on the other side of the very line they were defending.

Which is why the decisive thing about a company is no longer what it makes. It's the question it asks.

Encyclopedia Britannica asked: how do we sell one more set?

Wikipedia asked: how do we give one more person access?

The next great companies will ask a question neither of them had any reason to ask: **how much can we pay each person to use the thing we give away — and still win?**

The first question built the industrial age. The second built the open internet. The third has barely been asked out loud — and it's the one this book is about.

### Beyond Zero

For most of human history, free was unimaginable. For the last twenty years, free has been ordinary. This book is about the next twenty — what happens when the latch finally turns, and the door swings the other way.

Almost nothing on the far side of that door is new. The trucks exist. The solar panels exist. The advertising that funds free products has funded them at planetary scale for decades. The QR code that turns a bottle into a storefront dates back to 1994. What was missing was never the technology. It was the idea that the technology could be pointed past free — and the one operator willing to build as if it could.

So the question stops being the one every business has asked since the first market — how much should we charge? — and becomes one almost no one has asked: how much can we give, and still win? That's not a softer question. It's a harder one. The gift can't be a subsidy bleeding in from somewhere else, the way Caesar's grain was. It has to be funded by the business itself — paid for out of its own efficiency and the value it captures — or it collapses back into charity. And it's a more competitive question, because once a rival has solved it — once a rival is paying customers to take what you sell — charging them is no longer an option you get to keep. The race that scarcity ran toward the highest price runs, on the other side of zero, toward the deepest gift. Same engine. Opposite direction.

None of this arrives all at once, and none of it arrives on its own. The scarcity that ruled most of history was real — physics, not choice. The drought, the empty mine, the work only one pair of hands could do. But the scarcity we now enforce on things that have already become abundant is something else entirely: a design choice, inherited and rarely examined. Unmaking it is work — system by system, industry by industry, each crossing built by someone who reached the open door and decided, against every instinct the old model trained into them, to walk through it. The chapters ahead are that work: how a free bottle of water can pay for the truck that delivers it, how energy funds itself, how data turns honest, how a single building can supply a city and pay it. The door is open. Almost no one has walked through. The rest of this book is about what's on the other side — and how to get there.

## Chapter 2: Crossing Zero: The Map from Scarcity to Abundance

For two decades, some of the smartest thinkers about economic abundance have been chasing the same horizon. Chris Anderson wrote *Free* in 2009. Jeremy Rifkin wrote *The Zero Marginal Cost Society* in 2014. A whole generation of technologists, economists, and futurists landed on the same claim: as technology advances, the cost of making goods and services will keep falling toward zero. Information wants to be free. Software wants to be free. Eventually, they argued, much of the physical economy would follow. *Free* became the horizon. The whole post-scarcity conversation came down to one question: what does the world look like when the price of everything that can be copied, digitized, or automated falls to nothing?

They were right about the direction. They were wrong about the destination. Zero is not where this ends. Zero is where it begins. Competition between free goods and services will push entire industries below free. That is what it means to cross zero: not to arrive at free, but to evolve beyond it.

Picture a simple number line — the kind from a math class. On the right side of zero sit the systems built on scarcity: resources are limited, competition is fierce, and value flows upward toward whoever controls the land, the capital, or the infrastructure. On the left side sit the systems built on abundance: resources circulate beyond free, participation creates new value, and the benefits spread outward instead of piling up at the top. Right in the middle sits zero — the point where price disappears. Everything to the right of zero costs money. Everything to the left of zero pays you to participate.

For thousands of years, humanity has built every major economic system on the right side of that line — or, just recently, right at zero. This chapter is about what happens when we stop treating zero as a ceiling to reach for and start treating it as a doorway to cross.

### The Systems That Preceded Zero

Here's the observation most economic commentary misses. Communism, socialism, capitalism, and the sharing economy are usually described as completely different systems. And each has real strengths. Capitalism has created more total wealth, and turned new technology into products faster, than any system ever has. Socialism has delivered broad access to basics, and often better results than pure markets in health, education, and housing. Centrally planned economies proved that large-scale coordination — literacy campaigns, industrialization, basic infrastructure — is possible outside of markets. The sharing economy showed how digital platforms could put idle capacity to work: empty seats, empty rooms, empty hours.

Each has also failed in its own signature way. Capitalism produces homelessness next to extreme wealth, and tolerates planned obsolescence, pollution, and rent extraction. Socialism slows down without the productive base that markets build. Centrally planned systems struggled to handle information at scale and concentrated power catastrophically — though hybrids like modern China have beaten the pure versions of both systems on certain measures. The sharing economy promised distributed access and delivered powerful platform monopolies that squeeze value out of workers and customers alike.

The strengths are real. The failures are real. But every one of these systems — including the best-performing versions — still operates on the right side of zero. They argue about who pays, who controls, and who benefits. They never question the deeper assumption underneath all of them: that value, at scale, must flow through positive-priced exchange. And wherever it does, power piles up somewhere. These systems share one more trait: persistently low aggregate efficiency — enormous resources burned for the value actually produced. Not because anyone wanted waste, but because structures built for control or competition treat waste as an acceptable cost. Two centuries of political argument, revolution, and war have all played out inside this one narrow band of economic possibility: the zone to the right of zero.

**The Free Economy** was the first real break. Free search, free email, free social media, free streaming, open-source software, ad-supported news — all of it operates at zero. The user pays nothing; someone else covers the cost. Billions of people now search, message, watch, and scroll all day long without paying for any of it at the point of use. For the first time at global scale, an economy proved that price is not required for value to exist. That was the first crack in the wall of scarcity.

Some of that first wave operated honestly at zero. Some of it only looked free while quietly extracting. Wikipedia gives you information and asks nothing back. Facebook appears to do the same but takes more than it gives — a distinction that becomes central in later chapters. But here's what's true of both, and of every free platform that scaled: they moved the axis. They demolished the assumption that scale requires transactions. Without that demolition, nothing further left on the number line would have been imaginable — let alone buildable.

The Free Economy stops at zero. It sits at the center of the number line: the consumer loses nothing, and gains nothing extra either. It is not the destination. It is the doorway. The destination is on the other side.

**The Giving Economy** begins there — products and services that are not just free but negatively priced.

A negatively priced system is one whose structure generates enough value through participation to send surplus back out. **The consumer pays nothing — and on top of that, either receives a payment back or funds a cause they care about, just by participating.** That is the left side of zero.

This is not consumer surplus — the economist's term for the extra value you feel you got beyond the price you paid. It is not a subsidy, which depends on outside money. And it is not simply an ad-supported model. A negatively priced system is structurally different from all three. The surplus is generated inside the system, by the architecture itself — through participation, efficiency, and network effects — and it flows outward as a built-in feature, not a marketing gesture.

And unlike every system before it, the Giving Economy is not one more variation inside the same zone. It is a different zone entirely. The transition of our lifetimes is not between any two of the right-side systems. It is between all of them, together, and what becomes possible past the boundary none of them ever crossed.

### Where the Value Comes From

Every product sits somewhere on the number line, and every product can be moved along it. There are only two ways to do the moving. Capture more value — absorb money and resources from places the industry doesn't normally use. Or consume fewer resources — cut the real cost of producing and delivering the thing. Pull either lever and the product travels left. Whether it travels far enough to cross is a separate question, and the answer is not the same for both. Pull both and they compound: efficiency shrinks the cost, capture grows the revenue, each round of one funding the next round of the other. That loop mostly governs how far a system travels, not whether it crosses. New tactics will keep appearing. They are not new levers, only new ways of pulling those two.

But one of the two is doing three different jobs, and nothing in that pairing tells you how to tell them apart. Value capture is not a single move. It is three, drawing on three unrelated sources — and an operator who knows only one of them will conclude the framework doesn't apply the moment their category lacks it.

So, before the worked examples begin, here is the whole map of where the value can come from.

**Efficiency lowers the real cost of any good, but it cannot by itself produce a negative price while irreducible inputs remain. Once efficiency reaches that floor, crossing requires value from somewhere other than the user: from the system's own output, from the good's surroundings, or from the participant.**

That is the sorting rule. Every crossing in this book draws on those four things. I have not found a fifth.

**Lowering the floor.** The cost surface does most of the work — on an ordinary essential, roughly seven-eighths of the distance to zero, as the next chapter walks through in full. Remove the intermediaries. Produce locally. Split the cost of the trip across every stop on a route rather than charging it to one delivery. Treat the household's own shelf as the final warehouse. Bring the links in-house, one at a time. Every cent removed is a cent that never has to be earned again.

And then it stops. Beneath the waste sits real input that costs real money, and no amount of subtraction removes it. Efficiency is what makes the crossing affordable. It is not what makes the crossing.\[^1\]

**Monetizing what the system already makes.** The second source is the system's own production — part of it sold so that the rest can be given away. A site can sell its primary output at the ordinary market price and put the proceeds back into producing more of it. It can convert that output on site into something worth more and sell that instead. And it can sell what the process throws off along the way: a facility generating more power than it consumes sells the excess, a process throwing off heat sells that heat into a district grid rather than venting it, water recovered beyond what a site needs goes back to the municipal supply. In each case what is being sold is not a surface, a sponsorship, or a claim on anyone's attention. It is the ordinary output of the system, sold at an ordinary price, and the proceeds fund what the system hands over for nothing.

Notice what is absent from every route in this class. No advertiser. No sponsor. No commission. A system here can cross on nothing but what it natively produces — and Chapter 14 works that case in full.

**Capturing what sits around the good.** The third source is everything adjacent to the product that someone other than the user will pay for. A printed surface is one of them — a bottle held for an hour is a billboard someone will rent. But the surface is one branch of this class, not the class itself, and an operator who stops there has run the search once rather than properly.

Ask who else benefits from this good reaching this person. A retailer whose for-sale goods travel the same road to the same door benefits, and can pay for the trip that carries them both. A purchase the household makes downstream through the same channel returns a commission — and a commission is a cut of commerce, not a draw on any advertising budget. An operator with its own catalogue can use the surface to sell its own goods, competing with no one for budget because no external budget is involved. Routes can be sponsored. Employers can fund what their workers use. The data a system generates as it runs can be licensed to the cities and retailers that want it.

Twenty cents of irreducible cost, ten cents to a cause, five cents handed back to the household: the outside funding requirement on an ordinary essential comes to about thirty-five cents a unit. Several of these sources appear capable of carrying that on their own.

**Routing what the participant produces.** The fourth source is the person. A participant watching an advertisement produces value. So does one writing a review, building an audience, making something ten million people see. So does one contributing an hour, a completed task, an expertise someone needs — and that second family requires no advertiser in the loop at all.

How much of that range a system converts is not fixed. It is a property of the architecture. A thin system converts only attention. A wide one converts the full spread of what a person can produce — which is why a good whose own packaging earns nothing can still be better than free. In this book's preferred form that credit is portable, belonging to the participant rather than to the platform. That is a design choice, not a property that arrives on its own — and a price of zero is not, by itself, evidence that a system has crossed.

**What sits underneath.** Five things support all four classes without being sources of value for the initial crossing. Settlement makes what a person earned in one place spendable in another. Verification proves the claims. Reinvestment — the discipline of holding surplus rather than paying it out — compounds the result; except in the one case the footnote below describes, it funds what follows a crossing rather than causing one. The User Test decides whether the crossing was real from the participant's side. And expression — the donation, the payment handed back — is how the negative price becomes visible to the person receiving it. The donation is not what funds the giveaway. It is what the giveaway looks like.

| **Lower the floor**                      | **Monetize native output**       | **Capture adjacent value**    | **Route participant value** |
|------------------------------------------|----------------------------------|-------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| Intermediary removal                     | Primary output at market         | Printed surface (advertising) | Attention → credit          |
| Local production and consecutive routing | Converted output at higher value | Downstream commission         | Contribution → credit       |
| Circular inputs                          | Recovered byproduct sold         | Route-share fee               | Creation → credit           |

*Representative routes from each class. The fuller map is in the appendix.*

This book will spend more time on the printed surface than on anything else in that table — for reasons of evidence, not importance. It is the branch I have run at scale, and can therefore cost honestly rather than describe.

The framework is the grid. The bottle is one cell of it.

\[^1\]: One special case is worth naming. A system whose output can be reinvested into reproducing its own productive capacity does not so much exhaust its cost surface as lower it: energy from a generation system can power the manufacture, transport and installation of additional generation, where a bottle does nothing to make the next bottle. Such a system can build each increment more cheaply than the last, and so arrives at the crossing with less left to fund. It is also the one case where reinvestment contributes to the crossing itself rather than only to what follows it. The arithmetic in Chapter 14 does not model this — it holds cost per watt constant — which makes those timelines conservative by an unstated margin.

### The User Test

The formal definition of a negatively priced product — free to the user, plus either a direct payment or a donation made on their behalf — is necessary but not sufficient. A system can satisfy that structure on paper and still fail to cross zero in real life.

Take the extreme case: a free product that donates ten cents per unit to a cause the person despises — a repressive regime, a terrorist group, anything that actively works against what they value. By the formal definition, that product qualifies as negatively priced. No reasonable person would accept that conclusion. The donation doesn't benefit the person; it attacks what they care about. On paper, the system crossed zero. In reality, it didn't.

The same principle applies in subtler cases. Facebook is free. Meta gives money to charity. On paper, that looks like a free-plus-donation product. But run an honest accounting of the average user's experience — the hours of attention captured, the behavioral data harvested and sold, the documented effects on mental health, the outrage amplified — and what's taken dwarfs what's returned. The occasional donation doesn't reverse the direction the value flows. "If it's free, you're the product" is the everyday version of that math.

That means the left side of zero is not just a spot on a price axis. It's a judgment: does the system, on net, deliver more value to the person than it extracts? **A product only truly operates past zero when an ordinary user, looking back on the experience, feels they came out ahead.**

Ecosia passes that test. FreeWater passes that test. Facebook does not.

That verdict is a distribution, not a unanimity. FreeWater passes for the large majority of people who receive it, and not for all — someone who objects to the packaging, or to advertising in their kitchen, or to the cause the ten cents funds, isn't better off by their own accounting, and theirs is the only accounting this test recognizes. For them the product sits at zero rather than below it: free, and no more than free. That's the despised-cause example turned down from a reduction to an ordinary preference. It also points at a design response rather than a limitation — Chapter 10 shows what happens when the system stops assigning the cause and lets the participant direct it, which is the same test with the dissenting case engineered out.

Engineering a negatively priced system isn't a formula. It's a design problem. A company can check every formal box of left-of-zero and still build something the market correctly recognizes as extractive. Systems that pass the User Test compound and earn trust. Systems that fail it pile up skepticism — no matter how elegant the financial structure looks on paper.

### The Depths Already Reached

Negatively priced systems aren't speculation. They already exist, and they're already operating well past zero. The current floor isn't the model's limit — it's just where the first builders stopped.

Ecosia, a search engine that works like Google, directs 100% of its profits to climate action — most of it tree planting. Because one more search costs almost nothing to serve, its ad revenue has funded more than 200 million trees. By any reasonable accounting, the environmental value created far exceeds what the service costs to run. The system runs deep into negative territory.

FreeWater's arc was more gradual. The first campaign delivered free water plus a ten-cent donation to clean-water projects per bottle — the equivalent of -110% off the price of a comparable paid bottle. That wasn't the theoretical ceiling. It was the floor, set by total inexperience: no background in beverages, advertising, manufacturing, or software; no capital; a supply chain outsourced to middlemen at every step. Every available mistake got made. And the model still worked. Later campaigns, including the \#TeamWater effort with MrBeast, reached -130%. As the architecture improved, the system moved further left.

Even more telling is the replication. Free Paani, a Pakistani startup launched by an early reader of the operational manual, gave away over a million negatively priced bottles in its first year. In a market with roughly 1/460th the per-person advertising spend of the United States, the model didn't just cross zero — ad engagement per bottle ran higher than in U.S. campaigns. In less saturated markets, attention itself is worth more. That pattern has consequences the later chapters on transition will come back to.

Now imagine a version of FreeWater that owns its factories, runs its own routes through consecutive delivery and negatively priced vending machines, and fully stacks its revenue layers. It would push dramatically further into negative territory. The theoretical ceiling per unit is far higher than the early results suggest. Once a system crosses zero, the value it can return is no longer bound by traditional pricing logic. The constraint was never wealth. It was design.

### What Unlocks Past Zero

As systems move deeper past zero, new capabilities appear — things that were structurally impossible while products had to be sold at a positive price. A few are worth naming now. Others get whole chapters later.

Consecutive delivery becomes viable. When there's no payment to collect and no returns to manage at every stop, products can flow straight from production to the consumer along local, personalized routes. At SXSW 2022 in Austin, a team of four delivered 4,800 bottles — two hundred full 24-packs — door to door in under sixty minutes. Traditional shipping would have cost thousands of dollars. Consecutive delivery didn't just erase that cost — it paid each worker ten cents per unit. The model only works once price is removed as the central constraint.

Past zero, distribution itself becomes addressable. When people are compensated for participating, they willingly identify themselves, schedule pickups, and opt in. A negatively priced product isn't left at a generic kiosk for whoever wanders by — it reaches a known, verified recipient, matched with ads and offers that actually fit them. Advertisers stop buying uncertain impressions and start paying for guaranteed, high-attention placement. That precision raises the revenue per unit — which the system can hand back as even deeper negative pricing. The architecture and the revenue model reinforce each other in a loop that neither traditional retail nor simple free giveaways can copy.

Whole new business architectures become possible. Packaging turns into a dynamic interface. Delivery vehicles become mobile advertising and commerce platforms. Vending machines customize in real time. Homes become distributed warehouse nodes. These aren't incremental upgrades. They're fundamentally new designs, and they only become viable once the cost constraint that locked every component into its cheapest possible form is finally removed.

The deeper a system moves past zero, the more of these unlocks appear. Each one compounds the surplus. Each new surplus funds deeper motion leftward. The number line isn't just a map of price. It's a map of possibility — of what becomes buildable once the economic floor drops below zero.

### The Two Types of Competition

Competition doesn't disappear when an economy crosses zero. It changes form.

On the right side of zero, competitive position is zero-sum. Money must be extracted from the buyer at the point of sale, so companies fight over a finite pie. If one firm gains share, another loses it. The smartest moves are often not innovation but control — of supply, shelf space, patents, distribution channels, customer relationships. Scarcity systems naturally produce gatekeepers and toll collectors. Not because the people are malicious, but because the rules reward whoever best captures and defends scarce resources. The mechanism varies — central authority, capital, the platform — but the orientation never does.

On the left side of zero, the scoreboard flips. When participation itself creates value, winning no longer means grabbing a bigger slice of a fixed pie. It means growing the pie for everyone. The strongest competitor is the one who creates the most total value per participant: better outcomes for the user, bigger benefits for communities, stronger returns for the network. **Right-side competition rewards capture. Left-side competition rewards giving.**

The difference is concrete. If a competitor enters your city offering fifteen cents per bottle in donations while you offer ten, you can't beat them by charging less — you can't charge less than free. You beat them by getting more efficient, layering in new revenue models, and donating twenty. The ambition of competition doesn't vanish. Its direction reverses.

A system that pays its users can be harder to compete with than one that charges them.

### The Invisible Wall Around Zero

If the left side of zero is so powerful, an obvious question follows: why hasn't the world already moved there? The answer isn't technology. It isn't resources. It isn't even economics. The real barrier is the infrastructure built around one assumption: that products must be sold at a positive price.

Markets, accounting rules, tax codes, supply chains, banks, corporate boards — all of them bake that assumption in at the deepest level. Factories are built to make goods that will be sold. Stores are built to collect payment at checkout. Accounting tracks revenue per unit sold. Taxes are collected on sales. Capital markets reward businesses built around positive-priced sales and exits. None of this is merely unhelpful to negative pricing — it actively assumes negative pricing doesn't exist. The infrastructure isn't neutral. It's load-bearing for the right side of zero.

There's also a softer reason the wall has held longer than the technology required. Competition in any new zone runs through phases. Phase one is reaching the zone — getting from positive prices to free. Phase two is feature competition inside the zone — speed, scope, network effects, better interfaces, anything that separates one free product from another. Phase three is crossing the next boundary — from free to negatively priced. Most of the Free Economy is still in phase two. Feature competition still works; search, social, email, maps, and streaming have consolidated by building better products, not by crossing zero. But as features saturate — as each new one matters less to users — the next available competitive move changes. The first operator to cross zero in a saturated free category gains a moat no competitor can match with faster shipping. Crossing zero at high efficiency requires rebuilding infrastructure, and that's a multi-year structural advantage no incumbent can sprint to neutralize.

When a product becomes free, the infrastructure has to be partly rebuilt to get the most out of it. The Free Economy did this for digital goods — replacing point-of-sale revenue with advertising, sponsorship, and platform monetization. Google, YouTube, Wikipedia, and the rest now run inside a parallel infrastructure that took the legacy systems decades to accommodate. When a product becomes negatively priced, the rebuild goes deeper. Value circulates in several directions at once — engagement, data, network effects, charitable flow, efficiency gains — and the old infrastructure was never designed to measure, govern, or capitalize on value that moves sideways and outward instead of upstream toward a seller.

That is the invisible wall. It isn't physical. It's structural. And it's already cracking. The Free Economy proved digital industries could route around it. The current generation of negatively priced systems is testing whether the same route works for physical goods. As renewable energy approaches the point where each extra unit of power costs almost nothing to produce, as automated logistics and decentralized platforms mature, as data infrastructure becomes addressable, the conditions for negative pricing in physical industries fall into place. The wall around zero was never meant to be permanent. It was just the boundary of the systems we inherited. And like every piece of infrastructure before it, it can be rebuilt.

### A Pricing Map Is Not Enough

The number line maps price. But price alone can't tell you whether a system on the left side is truly regenerative or just subsidized. It can't tell you whether the user is actually better off. It can't tell you whether the system is compounding or leaking. For that, you need a different measurement entirely — one that asks not "what does this cost?" but "how much value does this system produce compared to what it consumes?"

That's the question the next chapter answers.

# Part 2: THE MECHANICS OF ABUNDANCE

## Chapter 3: Aggregate Efficiency

In 1989, the Exxon Valdez spilled eleven million gallons of crude oil into Prince William Sound. The spill killed hundreds of thousands of animals, devastated local fisheries, and contaminated more than a thousand miles of Alaskan coastline. It also raised U.S. GDP by several billion dollars. The cleanup crews, the legal fees, the compensation payments, the new equipment, the added medical costs — all of it counted as economic activity. By the metric the world still uses to measure economic success, the Exxon Valdez was good for the American economy.

That one fact tells you everything you need to know about why we need a different metric.

GDP measures how much money moved. It doesn't measure how much value was created. It doesn't notice when the activity is spent fixing a problem that never needed to exist. A factory that pollutes a river and a nonprofit that cleans it up both count as growth. A traffic jam that burns fuel and a delivery system that ends the traffic jam both register as economic output. By GDP's math, getting sick and paying a hospital to treat you is more productive than staying healthy in the first place.

These aren't edge cases. They are the measurement itself.

The world needs a different gauge. Not because GDP is wrong, but because it answers the wrong question. GDP tells you how busy the economy is. It doesn't tell you whether the economy is getting better or worse at turning the resources it burns into the outcomes people actually need.

That's the question behind aggregate efficiency.

### What Aggregate Efficiency Measures

Aggregate efficiency asks one question: how much value does a system create, compared to the honestly counted cost of creating it?

The term is borrowed on purpose. In thermodynamics and energy economics, *aggregate efficiency* means useful work divided by total energy consumed — and the central finding of that field is that most systems leave a huge gap between how they actually perform and the ceiling physics allows. This book extends the word to its economic cousin: value over honestly counted cost. The borrowing works because the gap has the same shape in both worlds, and closing it is what funds the next thing — either way. (The energy chapters use the physical version directly; a later chapter traces where the economic version parts ways with it.) When this book says aggregate efficiency without a label, it means the economic version.

And "honestly counted" means something specific: every cost on the page — including the ones the market lets a system push onto other people. "Value" here can mean two different things — does the system survive honest accounting, and does it leave the world better off — and the next section separates them. For now, hold the single idea: value over honest cost. Every action in an economy uses resources. Energy powers machines. Materials become products. Human time and effort turn ideas into services and infrastructure. Traditional metrics focus on output — how many goods got made, how much money changed hands, how much activity happened. Aggregate efficiency focuses on the relationship between the value created and the honest cost of creating it. A system with high aggregate efficiency produces more value for the world per honest dollar of cost — and it doesn't survive by quietly billing its costs to someone outside the ledger.

Consider two factories producing the same bottled water. On their books, the first looks like the better business: higher margins, lower costs, larger profit. It runs on cheap grid power, buys the cheapest packaging and throws out what it can't sell, and pays its workers below a living wage. The second earns less on paper: renewable power costs more up front, its packaging is optimized and reclaimed rather than dumped, almost nothing gets discarded, and its workers earn a living wage. By every number that reaches a shareholder, the first factory wins.

Now put the missing costs on the page. The first factory's cheap power carries carbon it never paid for. Its dumped packaging becomes waste someone downstream absorbs. The gap between what it pays its workers and a living wage is a cost carried by those workers and the public — not by the factory. Price all of that honestly, and the first factory's profit was always partly an illusion: it was profitable because its costs were being paid by people outside its accounting. The second factory consumes less of what it doesn't pay for, so under honest accounting it produces more real value per dollar of true cost. The ranking flips. The factory that looked worse on its books is the one actually creating value for the resources it consumes — and the one that looked better was extracting the difference.

That flip depends on a move the next section has to make carefully: deciding what counts as a cost, and at what price. Those aren't free choices, and the metric doesn't pretend they are. What counts, where the boundary sits, and what price a ton of carbon or an hour below a living wage carries — all of it has to be declared and defended in the open. That's the metric's discipline, and it's the opposite of what GDP does. GDP hides its boundary and counts whatever crosses a market price. Aggregate efficiency forces the boundary and the prices into view, where they can be argued with. The factory flip is not a thumb on the scale. It's what happens when costs that were already being paid — just paid by someone else — get named and priced where everyone can see them.

That flip is also what separates aggregate efficiency from ordinary operating efficiency. A plain efficiency measure would have preferred the first factory — it wastes less of what it pays for. Aggregate efficiency asks the harder question: waste less of *what*, counted *how*? Once the externalities — the costs pushed onto others — are on the page, "efficient" and "profitable" stop being the same word.

And this becomes decisive as economies move toward abundance. In scarcity systems, growth comes from producing more. In abundance systems, progress comes from creating more value per unit of honest cost. The goal shifts from maximizing activity to maximizing that ratio. And as the ratio rises, participation expands anyway, because the constraint of price starts to lift.

#### The Formula

At its core, aggregate efficiency asks: does this system produce more value than it consumes, once all the costs are honestly counted? The formula expresses that question in two forms, because there are two different things you can ask about a system.

**The Market Form** asks whether the system would survive on its own terms if it had to pay the costs it currently pushes onto others:

Aggregate Efficiency (Market) = (Value produced in the market − Cost of externalities) ÷ Total cost consumed

This is not a description of current performance. It's a description of what current performance *would be* if the accounting reflected reality. Most systems that look profitable today would fail this test if it were applied rigorously — which is exactly the point. The Market Form has a hard, meaningful break-even at one. Below it, the system only appears to pay its way because someone outside the ledger is covering the difference. Above it, the system genuinely funds itself.

**The Social Form** asks a broader, softer question — not "does it survive honest accounting" but "does it leave the world better off":

Aggregate Efficiency (Social) = (Value produced in the market + Consumer and social benefit − Cost of externalities) ÷ Total cost consumed

The two forms give different answers for the same system, and the difference is the point. A fast-food chain might clear the Social Form on the cheap calories it delivers while failing the Market Form once its health and environmental costs are priced. A public library fails the Market Form badly — it sells almost nothing — and clears the Social Form by an enormous margin. Reading both tells you what *kind* of value a system produces: whether it pays for itself, whether it benefits the world, or both.

One caution the rest of the chapter depends on: the two forms do not behave the same way at the threshold, and pretending they do is how this metric gets misused. The Market Form has a true knife-edge at one, because the top and bottom of its fraction are the same kind of thing — dollars in, dollars out — so "above one" means "funds itself" in a strict sense. The Social Form has no clean knife-edge, because its top line includes consumer benefit and social welfare, which can run very large and are hard to pin down. A free essential can post a Social Form score of ten or fifty without that meaning anything like "fifty times self-funding." The Market Form answers *can this system pay its own way and grow* — a precise question with a real break-even. The Social Form answers *does this system make the world better* — a directional question. A sign, not a ratio to take literally. When this book asks whether a system "crosses one," it means the Market Form unless it says otherwise.

### What the formula is, and is not

The formula is not new mathematics. It's the same benefit-cost reasoning public economists have used since the 1930s, applied with modern tools for pricing the things markets don't price — economists call this shadow pricing — and a clear rule for where the boundary sits. What it contributes isn't arithmetic. It's discipline: a specific way of using those tools to answer one question — is this system, as designed, building more than it breaks?

So it's a gauge, not a precise readout. It doesn't produce a single number that settles every argument, and it doesn't pretend to. Two honest analysts using different but defensible prices for carbon will get different values for the same system. The metric's response is not to hide that, but to require it: what counts as a cost, where the boundary sits, and what price each externality carries all have to be declared in the open, where they can be argued with. That declaration *is* the method. A number you can interrogate is worth more than a number that hides its assumptions — which is exactly GDP's failing. GDP counts whatever crosses a market price and never tells you what it left out.

What the formula prevents is the easy thing: ignoring the costs that never reach the ledger. Once the externalities are on the page, a system can no longer hide behind its market price.

### A worked case

Abstract formulas invite suspicion, so here is one run end to end — the conventional data center from a later chapter, costed two ways. The figures are round on purpose, chosen to show the structure rather than to pin down any real facility's books. What matters is the shape of the flip, not the exact dollars.

On its own books, the data center is profitable. Call it \$100 million a year in value produced — computing power sold, services delivered — against \$80 million in costs it actually pays: hardware, electricity at grid rates, staff, the building. By the only accounting it currently faces, its ratio is 100 ÷ 80 = **1.25**. A healthy business.

Now put the missing costs on the page. The electricity it buys at grid rates carries carbon it doesn't pay for. Price that carbon at the EPA's published social cost — the government's official estimate of the damage each ton causes — and the figure lands somewhere around \$15 million a year. The water it draws for cooling comes from a stressed watershed at below replacement cost; price the gap and add roughly \$5 million. Together, that's \$20 million of cost the data center causes but doesn't carry.

That \$20 million does two separate jobs, and it's worth being exact that this is not the same dollar counted twice. It raises the bottom of the fraction: true cost consumed is \$100 million, not \$80 million, because the system used up atmosphere and watershed it never paid replacement cost for. And it lowers the top: the honest value produced is \$100 − \$20 = \$80 million, because that same consumption produced harm, not value. One correction fixes what the system spent. The other fixes what it made. They happen to be the same size only because, here, the underpriced input and the damage it caused are the same physical thing. The Market ratio is now 80 ÷ 100 = 0.80. The same facility that looked like a 1.25 business is a 0.80 system once the costs it pushes outward are counted. It was never funding itself. It was being subsidized — by the atmosphere and the watershed — to the tune of the gap.

Notice what the worked numbers force into the open. Change the carbon price and the result moves. At a low social cost of carbon, the carbon figure might be \$8 million instead of \$15 million — total externalities of \$13 million, a ratio near 0.94. Still below one, still extractive, but closer to the line. At a high carbon price, the total externality climbs toward \$30 million, and the ratio sinks toward 0.64. That sensitivity isn't a flaw to hide. It's the honest band around the answer, and it's exactly why the prices have to be declared. But notice what the band does *not* do: across the entire defensible range of carbon prices, the data center stays below one. The assumptions move the *exact ratio.* They don't move the *verdict*. That's the difference between a system near the threshold, where the pricing genuinely decides the question, and a system like this one, where the gap is so large that no defensible set of prices rescues it. The formula is least certain about the systems closest to one — and most certain about exactly the systems that matter most to catch.

### The Threshold at One

That worked case is the whole chapter in one number. The line where the Market ratio equals one is the boundary between a system that funds itself and a system being fed from somewhere outside its own accounting.

Below one, the system consumes more than it produces. It can't sustain itself on its own output, so it must be fed — by taxes, by subsidy, by unpriced environmental damage, by unpaid labor, by future debt. Most of the scarcity economy lives here, and most of it looks profitable, because the feeding is invisible: the costs have been pushed onto people or ecosystems outside the measurement boundary. The data center looked like 1.25 for exactly this reason. Pull the boundary wide enough to catch what it pushes outward, and the apparent surplus turns out to have been a subsidy all along.

Coal is the century-long version of the same story. Coal power looked profitable for a hundred years — cheap electricity, healthy margins, the foundation of industrial growth — and it looked that way because it never paid for the lungs it damaged, the climate it altered, or the landscapes it tore open. Put those on the page, at any honest price, and coal crosses below one by a wide margin. It was never a regenerative system. It was an extractive one whose extraction happened to be invisible to the only ledger anyone was keeping.

At one, the system is in balance: it covers its own maintenance but produces no surplus to expand, distribute, or give away. Above one, it produces genuine surplus — and that surplus is what the rest of this book is built on. A system that funds itself can fund its own growth, return value to participants, or carry a donation, without depending on anything outside its boundary.

Here a distinction has to be made carefully, because it's the one most easily fumbled. **Aggregate efficiency tells you the sign, not the speed.** It tells you *whether* a system produces surplus — which side of one it sits on. That's a question about direction: regenerative or extractive. It does not, by itself, tell you how fast a system grows. Growth depends on a separate quantity: how much of the surplus gets kept and reinvested, measured against the capital base it's reinvested into. A system can sit comfortably above one and grow slowly, because it pays its surplus out. Another can sit just above one and grow fast, because it plows everything back. The ratio is the sign. The reinvestment of the surplus is the speed. They're different numbers answering different questions, and the chapters ahead keep them apart: this chapter establishes whether a system is on the regenerative side of the line; the later chapters on value chains and energy show how fast a system on that side can actually compound.

In practice, this means crossing one is necessary but not sufficient. A system below one can't compound at all — there's no surplus to reinvest, only a deficit to feed. A system above one *can* compound, but whether it does, and how fast, is a question the threshold doesn't answer. The threshold is the gate. What happens after the gate is the speed.

And the threshold is not a single fixed reading. Real systems oscillate. A regenerative system has bad cycles that dip below one; an extractive system has good cycles that rise above it. What matters isn't any single measurement but the long-run center of gravity — and which side of the line it sits on. A system averaging 0.95 across many cycles is extractive even when individual cycles look fine. One averaging 1.05 is regenerative even when individual cycles dip. The verdict is statistical, the threshold is real, and the question every later chapter ultimately asks is the same one: does this system, on average, sit on the regenerative side of one?

### How the Surplus Scales

The previous section drew the line: a system above one produces surplus, a system below one consumes it. That's the sign. This section is about the speed — once a system is on the regenerative side of the line, what determines how fast its surplus grows and how far it spreads? Two things do. The first is where the surplus comes from. The second is what happens when many systems share it.

Most discussions of efficiency look at one surface: revenue. How does the system earn more — new revenue streams, advertising, sponsorship, data, affiliate income the old version of the business couldn't reach? That surface is real; Chapter 2 named it as one of the two levers that move systems left of zero. But there's a second surface, and most economics underweights it.

Try a simple thought experiment. You can pay someone ten thousand dollars, or you can save them ten thousand dollars. Their bank balance ends up the same either way. But the mechanisms are not the same, and each has a property the other lacks. Revenue requires machinery. It needs a paying customer, an advertiser, an affiliate, a transaction — and the machinery that produces each dollar, the sales and marketing and fulfillment and collection, is itself a cost. Revenue scales, but the cost of generating it scales right alongside it. Cost savings carry no such baggage. Every dollar saved is a dollar that never has to be earned: no customer to win, no sponsor to approve, no advertiser to bid. It only requires that an inefficiency be removed — and once removed, the saving is permanent. Revenue has to be re-earned every cycle. A cost taken out stays out.

But the two surfaces are bounded differently, and the difference cuts against the obvious intuition. The cost surface is finite. There's only so much inefficiency in any system, and once it's been removed, that surface is used up — you can't keep cutting cost forever, because beneath the waste sits real input that costs real money, the floor the threshold section already named. The revenue surface has no such floor. It carries baggage, but it's renewable — more customers, more layers, recurring income that can grow as long as the system does. So the honest contrast is not that one surface eventually beats the other. It's that they have opposite shapes. The cost surface is high leverage but finite: each cut flows straight into surplus and never has to be re-won, but the well runs dry. The revenue surface is lower leverage but inexhaustible: every dollar carries the cost of earning it, but there's no point at which it stops.

This is why the cost surface matters most exactly as a system moves left of zero. A free or negatively priced product can't raise its price to absorb a cost increase — the price is already at or below zero — so every cost eliminated flows straight into the surplus: a deeper negative price, a larger donation, faster expansion. The cost surface is where the early, undiluted gains come from. The revenue surface is what carries the system once the easy cost is gone. A system that works both — taking out cost while it can, building revenue that lasts — grows its surplus faster than one with access to only the revenue side, the side a right-of-zero business captures as margin rather than passing through as surplus.

An example makes the difference concrete, and it is worth running because the two surfaces are usually collapsed into a single question about funding.

Take an ordinary domestic bottle of water, sold on a shelf for a dollar fifty. Walk the cost surface down, one removal at a time. Going direct removes the retailer's margin, the wholesaler's, and the distributor's. Producing locally removes the long-haul freight leg. Running a consecutive route splits the cost of the trip across every stop on it rather than charging it to a single delivery. Treating the household's own pantry as the final warehouse removes the regional distribution center and the retail shelf entirely. What is left is the cost of actually making the unit — and that cost falls too, as the operator brings links in-house: label manufacturing collapses toward the price of the ink, resin bought as bulk pellets falls with volume, water is drawn locally rather than shipped.

A finished, labeled, filled bottle bought from an outside vendor at modest volume runs about thirty cents — a published vendor price the value-chain chapter sources in full, and a ceiling rather than a floor, since nothing has been brought in-house. Driven down by the removals above, the same unit lands near twenty cents all in.

Now look at what those two numbers mean together. The cost surface carried the product from a dollar fifty to twenty cents. It did not cross zero, and it cannot, for the reason this section already gave: beneath the waste sits real input that costs real money. But it covered roughly seven-eighths of the distance.

That reframes the funding question entirely. A system that gives the unit away free, sends ten cents to a cause, and returns five cents to the household needs thirty-five cents per unit from somewhere other than the consumer. Not a dollar fifty. The cost surface already removed a dollar thirty of it.

Thirty-five cents is a low enough bar that several unrelated sources clear it independently. A commission on a single downstream purchase clears it. A third party paying to share a route already running past the door clears it. Credit a participant generated through contribution elsewhere clears it. Advertising clears it too — but advertising is one of the things that clears thirty-five cents, not the only one, and the chapters ahead cost the others.

Three honest limits hold this example to what it can bear. The proportions are category-specific: water sits near the favorable extreme, cheap to make, heavy to ship, and heavily marked up at retail, while a category with thin retail margin and expensive inputs will show a far smaller cost-surface share. The cost surface is finite, as this section has already argued — once removed, it is gone, which is why it buys the crossing but does not compound it. And the removals are not free to achieve: local production, owned routes, and in-house labeling take capital and years. Twenty cents is a destination the architecture can reach, not a condition an operator starts in — the distinction the next chapter draws between what a design can support and where an operator can honestly begin.

The second determinant appears only when many systems share a network — and it's where the speed stops being a property of any single product. Start with what a free or negatively priced essential becomes once it reaches everyone. Water, food, energy, transportation — each is something a person uses constantly, as part of daily life. When one of them crosses zero, it doesn't just reach a market segment. It reaches every household that needs it, on a dependable rhythm. At that point the product stops being a product and becomes a *touchpoint* — the most reliable, highest-frequency physical connection there is between a system and a person. Every day the packaging is seen, the code is scannable, the vehicle passes the door, the digital layer is reachable. A touchpoint that recurs daily across every household is no longer a good. It's infrastructure.

And infrastructure is a thing other things get built on. When one essential crosses zero, an ecosystem starts. When many essentials cross zero through the *same* network — the same households, the same delivery routes, the same packaging-as-interface, the same digital layer — the network itself becomes a platform, and the surplus of any one product stops being trapped inside that product.
