On the other side, competition does not stop — it changes form, from disruption-from-below into a contest over who can build and reach the deepest frontier their architecture allows, at the quality they intend. That contest has a winner every cycle, but it is won by being genuinely best, not by entering underneath an incumbent who cannot respond.

Jeremy Rifkin came the closest. Across four decades, most directly in *The Zero Marginal Cost Society* (2014), he traced what happens to economic life as technological efficiency rises: the trajectory from positive marginal cost, through steep decline, toward a floor near zero. And he described the *collaborative commons* that becomes viable once essential goods can be produced and shared at near-zero marginal cost. The aggregate-efficiency research he synthesized — the ratio of useful work to total energy consumed — showed the advanced economies climbing for most of the twentieth century, then plateauing in the 1990s. They stalled well below their thermodynamic ceiling. That stall is the empirical opening this book builds on, and his commons is the nearest thing in the prior literature to the world these chapters argue for. Where this book diverges is not in how far along the line it travels — to say Rifkin "stopped at zero" would only penalize him for not using a coordinate this book invented. The divergence is in the number of variables. Rifkin's account runs on one: the technology stack of communication, energy, and logistics, collapsing marginal cost toward zero, with abundance falling out the far end of the cost curve. But near-zero marginal cost is necessary, not sufficient — and the gap between the two is the whole subject of this book. A good can sit at near-zero cost and still extract: Meta's products cost almost nothing to serve and are paid for in attention and data the user never sees leave. The same near-zero cost can instead be architected into a good that pays the user and funds its own replacement. The cost curve does not decide which of those happens. The architecture does — and that architecture is the second variable. The mechanisms the earlier chapters developed — honest data, consecutive delivery, depth competition, regenerative value chains, the verifiable contribution built into the unit — are not properties of the technology stack but decisions made on top of it, and none of them appears in a calculation about marginal cost alone. They are what carry a low-cost good across zero rather than leaving it parked there as free-with-extraction. So the two frameworks are not rivals. They are different terms of the same equation — Rifkin's the cost trajectory, this book's the architectural variable that compounds with it. He described the descent toward zero with more rigor than anyone before him. This book describes what decides, once the descent arrives, whether a system extracts at the bottom or regenerates past it.

### Three Who Reached Differently

The thinkers in the first two sections share a single relationship to this book's argument: each mapped a zone, and this book extends past where their zone ended. The three below do not share that relationship, and so they are not grouped. Each stands in a different position. One described how a commons is governed, and is extended toward an architecture that needs less governing. One drew the *destination* an economy should reach, and is met with the engine that could move it there. And one proposed a different escape from the scarcity economy altogether — an escape this book does not take — and because some readers will assume the book is taking it, the contrast is worth stating plainly.

Elinor Ostrom dismantled an assumed inevitability. Garrett Hardin's 1968 argument held that a shared resource tends inevitably toward depletion — everyone draws on the common pasture until it collapses. Ostrom's *Governing the Commons* (1990), the work behind her 2009 Nobel, showed otherwise. Alpine pastures, irrigation districts, and coastal fisheries sustained themselves cooperatively for centuries — neither privatized nor state-run, held in balance by rules the users made and enforced themselves. Her eight design principles name the conditions under which that cooperation holds. This book shares her rejection of the private-versus-state binary and treats her finding as settled ground.

It does not claim to repeal rivalry. A fish caught is a fish gone; a pasture grazed is grass eaten. For genuinely rival, depletable resources, Ostrom's governance still does its full job, and nothing in the negative zone changes that. What changes is three things layered on top of it.

The first is rate. How fast a rival resource depletes is a function of how efficiently it is used, and left-of-zero systems are driven toward maximum efficiency — with no margin to hide waste in, every inefficiency is surplus that could have been returned and wasn't. The same aquifer, the same fishery, drawn on by a regenerative architecture, depletes more slowly than one drawn on by an extractive one. Ostrom's rules still govern the commons. They now govern a slower clock.

The second is direction. When giving is built into the architecture rather than bolted on as charity, a system can run *above* replacement on resources capable of regrowing. Ecosia is the existence proof: a negatively priced search engine whose structure routes surplus into reforestation has funded the planting of hundreds of millions of trees. That is a ceiling Ostrom's framework never reached — sustainable use could, at best, hold a commons steady; a giving-baked architecture can make it grow. The qualifier is real and load-bearing. This works only where nature can regenerate — forests, fisheries, soil, slowly recharging aquifers — and not at all for what cannot regrow: a drained ancient aquifer, an extinct species, a spent ore body. Those can only be depleted more slowly, not restored.

The third is scale, and it is a conditional, not a promise. If negatively priced architectures become a large share of economic activity — the adoption the book's predictions describe — then their combined effect, slower depletion across rival resources and net-positive regeneration across the resources that can recover, could begin to offset the damage that centuries of right-of-zero extraction left behind. The book claims the mechanism and the direction. Whether it reaches that scale is the open question every prediction in the next chapter puts at risk.

So the relationship to Ostrom is not extension into her territory but a layer beneath and beyond it. She described the governance that holds a fragile commons steady. This book describes an architecture that makes the commons deplete slower, lets the recoverable parts of it heal, and — at scale — could repay some of what was already taken. Her work answers how not to lose the commons. This one asks how to grow it back.

Kate Raworth is the closest contemporary predecessor. Her *Doughnut Economics* (2017) placed two boundaries around economic activity — a social foundation below which no one should fall, an ecological ceiling beyond which collective harm accumulates — and named the safe space for humanity as the ring between them. Her deepest insight is structural: that boundaries, not a growth figure, are the right unit of analysis. This book takes that insight and asks the question beneath it — what determines where the boundaries sit. Both are products of the architecture under them. The social floor sits where it does because the prevailing architecture leaves particular gaps in essential provision. The ecological ceiling sits where it does because that architecture extracts at a particular rate. Both move when the architecture changes.

Raworth drew the floor from human need — the internationally recognized minimums no one should fall beneath — not from what any economy could afford. What her frame leaves open is the engine: how a society actually lifts everyone above that floor without breaching the ceiling. In a right-of-zero world, the main engine available is redistribution. Redistribution draws on existing surplus — every dollar moved to the floor is taken from somewhere — so it scales only with the surplus an economy can spare, and progress toward the floor moves at the speed of that surplus. The negative zone adds a second engine entirely. When giving is baked into the unit, the floor rises with the *volume distributed* rather than with the surplus available to give away. Ten million negatively priced sandwiches that each route twenty-five cents to a cause is two and a half million dollars to that cause every year — a contribution with no counterpart in the for-sale world, where the same ten million sandwiches give nothing. The floor is no longer capped by what an economy can afford to redistribute. It scales with reach, and reach is a far larger and more elastic number. This holds even when the negative model is only modestly profitable, because the giving is attached to throughput, not charged against margin.

Profitability changes not whether the floor rises but how fast. A negatively priced model that is equally or more profitable than its for-sale counterpart funds its own expansion quicker — more routes, more production, more reach, sooner. And since volume is the multiplier on the per-unit giving, faster volume means a faster-rising floor. So the claim degrades gracefully: a less-profitable negative model still raises the floor, only more slowly, while a more-profitable one accelerates it. The per-unit giving is bounded by what the architecture can sustainably fund — the same value-capture frontier the earlier entries named — but within that bound, provision can rise far past what redistribution alone could fund. She drew the destination and made it legible to policymakers. This book describes an engine — one that reaches her floor by volume rather than by transfer, and keeps rising where redistribution plateaus. The two are not rivals. They are target and mechanism.

Karl Marx belongs here for a different reason than the others. Some readers will reach for him to place a book that says the prevailing economic architecture is pointed the wrong way and must be rebuilt, and the contrast is worth drawing precisely rather than leaving it loose. Marx centered class conflict and expropriation — the worker exploited by the owner, history advancing through struggle, the resolution a working class seizing the means of production and abolishing private ownership. This book shares almost none of that structure. It has no class antagonist and calls for no seizure. Its predictions describe incumbents and markets crossing zero because doing so out-competes extraction — operators moving themselves by incentive, not one class displacing another by struggle. And where Marx would abolish private ownership, this book runs the other way: it argues for ownership held widely, each participant holding a real share. It keeps prices, profit, competition, and voluntary exchange, and argues only that these have an unexplored region to the left of zero. There is one genuine convergence hidden inside that disagreement, and it is worth naming precisely so it is not mistaken for a larger one. Both visions end at a place where price no longer gates access to the essentials of life — and they reach it by opposite routes. Marx's end state removes price by removing the market: in full communism, money withers, and goods are distributed by need rather than exchange. This book removes price, for essentials, by driving it through zero inside the market — competition carried so far that the good comes to pay the person who uses it. The shared destination is narrow, and it is only that: for essentials, the price gate is gone. Everywhere else, the two could not be further apart. Marx abolishes price everywhere. This book preserves it as the very engine that does the work — and leaves the rest of the number line fully populated, a hundred dollars for a bottle of water and a thousand for a bottle of wine still sitting comfortably to the right of zero.

The one real point of contact is the diagnosis — value flows the wrong way — and the divergence is over why. Marx located exploitation in the structure of the market itself, so that only removing the structure removes the extraction. This book locates it more narrowly. There are two ways to win a customer. Give more value than you take — competing on price, quality, and service advanced together. Or win without giving more — on brand, lock-in, or a customer's inability to tell the difference. The first pushes a company left along the number line. The second is how value gets captured while the customer is held in place. Exploitation is not the nature of the market. It is the signature of the second way beating the first. And the pillars have to move together, because a single one can be improved by robbing another: a price cut funded by gutting quality, squeezing labor, or offloading cost onto the environment is extraction wearing a discount — and exactly the case where Marx looks right. The move that cannot be faked is cheaper *and* as good or better *and* as well-supported — fundable only by real efficiency or captured value, which is why aggregate efficiency, not good intention, is what carries a company left.

So the cure is not Marx's. The exploiter is not abolished; he loses, the ordinary way a worse competitor loses, to an operator who gives more and takes less. And once that competition runs past zero, the surplus returns to the user as payment or as a contribution made on their behalf. The market contains a door Marx never described, reached not by seizing the means of production but by competing through them. The honest limit is that none of it is automatic. Competition only pushes left where the customer can perceive the value, and where the robbed pillar stays hidden, capture wins — and the market looks exactly as exploitative as Marx said. Closing that gap is the book's wager — narrower and more testable than a revolution: force the competition onto real value, and the extraction Marx wanted a revolution to end is ended instead by the customer, choosing the operator who gives the most.

Three thinkers, three different relationships: a governance framework extended toward architecture, a destination met with an engine, and a rival escape route the book deliberately does not take. None is a boundary this book crosses. Each is a position this book defines itself against, or alongside, or in contrast to — which is a different and more various kind of relationship than the earlier sections described.

### Foundations, Not Boundaries

The thinkers in the first three sections were boundaries — frameworks this book extends past, or positions it defines itself against. The two that follow are different in kind. They are not boundaries the book crosses. They are foundations it stands on. One is a theory of information; one is a pair of physical principles. The book does not claim to reach past either. It claims only to add a question each was not built to ask — and in the case of the physics, to use it as the answer to an objection the book will otherwise repeatedly face.

Claude Shannon's *A Mathematical Theory of Communication* (1948) is the foundation under all modern digital communication. He defined information precisely — the reduction of uncertainty. He gave it a unit, the bit. He established the maximum rate at which a channel can reliably carry a signal, and he separated signal from noise. Data compression, error correction, and telecommunications all rest on what he built. This book does not extend Shannon; it uses him. Different economic architectures produce information channels with different properties, and Shannon's framework is the correct tool for evaluating the channels that result. Right-of-zero markets transmit a signal in which much of the bandwidth is taken up by budget constraint, rather than by the preference or value the signal is read as evidence of. Left-of-zero architectures produce channels with more room for those underlying signals, because the budget constraint is no longer inside the channel.

The one question the book adds is upstream of where Shannon's framework begins. Shannon takes the channel as given — the sender has a message, the channel exists, noise degrades it, and the question is how much gets through. He does not ask whether the architecture producing the channel has shaped which signals can exist in the first place. That was the right level of abstraction for a mathematical theory of communication, and it is not a flaw. But applied to economic information, it becomes load-bearing. A channel can transmit its signal faithfully and still misrepresent reality, if the architecture upstream distorted what could be expressed at all. A person who buys cheap bananas for months produces a perfectly faithful signal — and a false picture of what they actually prefer, because the architecture never offered conditions under which a truer pattern could form. Shannon remains the correct way to think about transmission once a channel exists. The book only adds that the architecture producing the channel is itself a design problem his framework was not built to engage.

The second law of thermodynamics, and the EROEI framework that applies it to energy, are the other foundation — and the most important one in this section, because the book leans on it as a defense. Energy Return on Energy Invested, developed by Charles Hall and others in the 1970s and 1980s, measures the ratio of energy a system produces to the energy required to build and run it. The crossing of the declining fossil-fuel curve and the rising renewable curve is one of the observations underneath the energy chapter. The book operates fully within EROEI's analytical domain. What EROEI does not measure is what happens to the surplus a positive-return system produces — whether it is routed back into the system's own maintenance and expansion, or extracted and consumed elsewhere. That routing is the difference between a well-performing extraction machine and a regenerative system, and it is the book's addition, not a correction of the framework.

The second law itself is worth meeting head-on, because some readers will reach for it as the disproof of any system described as producing more than it consumes. The second law states that the entropy of an isolated system — one exchanging nothing with its surroundings — does not decrease. The systems this book describes are open. They draw continuously on external inputs — sunlight, wind, geothermal gradients — that arrive whether or not anything is built to capture them. Open systems can produce local order while the wider universe runs down. This is not a violation of the second law but an application of what open systems are permitted to do — and what biology has done for four billion years. The distinction that resolves the objection is between *perpetual energy* and *perpetual economic operation*. Perpetual energy would violate thermodynamics: it would mean extracting more energy from an isolated system than it contained. Perpetual economic operation violates nothing, because the energy input is continuously replenished from outside the economic system. The architecture's job is twofold. Convert the arriving energy into economic output more efficiently than the alternatives do. Route the surplus through loops that cover the system's own maintenance and fund its expansion. The energy flow stays open; only the economic loop is closed. That is allowed, and it is the foundation the energy chapter rests on.

There is a reason these two are foundations and the earlier thinkers were boundaries, and it is worth stating plainly. Physics is not constructed. Energy flows, entropy increases, open systems produce local order — these operated before humans and are not ours to extend. Economics *is* constructed: money, markets, prices, and the categories that organize them are human inventions, and what was designed can be redesigned. The book's contribution sits exactly at that seam. It does not reach past Shannon or past the second law. It takes what physics permits — continuously replenished inputs, open systems producing local order, biology's long demonstration — and routes those physical allowances through economic structures that earlier frameworks were not built to support. The foundations are fixed. The architecture built on them is not.

## Chapter 19: What the Framework Predicts

The chapters before this one made an argument. This one puts it at risk.

A framework that only explains is hard to disprove and easy to dismiss — it can always absorb a new fact after the fact. A framework that *predicts* can be wrong, and being able to be wrong is what separates a theory from a story. So this chapter states what the framework expects to become observable, in specific industries, on a clock, in terms concrete enough to fail.

Each prediction below is a structural claim, not a bet on the specific companies it names. The framework describes a pressure — that competition, past zero, rewards giving over capture. Each prediction is that pressure applied to one industry: where it should surface first, what shape it should take, and what would count as the prediction failing. None of them claims certainty. Whether they hold depends on engineering, capital, and the choices of operators the framework does not control. What the framework claims is the *direction* of the pressure and the *form* the result should take, not the hand of any single player.

The predictions run on a roughly ten-to-twenty-five-year horizon. That is not evasion — it is the timescale the structural pressures actually operate on. Architecture this deep does not turn over in a quarter, and a prediction pretending otherwise would be less honest, not more. The clock starts at this book's first publication, and later editions do not reset it. Each is offered at the scale its claim actually lives at. And each ends with a failure line stated plainly enough that a reader can hold the framework to it.

### The Free Energy Leadership Prediction

Before energy crosses zero at national scale, the first proof units will appear in bounded zones: parking lots, campuses, ports, airports, truck stops, stadiums, and microgrids. These beachheads matter because they show the architecture working locally. But the true prediction begins when those sites link into networks and force countries to treat free or negatively priced energy as strategic infrastructure.

The first country to distribute fuel or energy at zero or negative cost across a meaningful portion of its economy will gain compounding advantages over countries where energy remains scarcity priced.

Energy is the base input beneath almost every other system: transportation, water, food, manufacturing, housing, compute, AI, logistics, cooling, heating, and defense. When energy remains expensive, every layer above it carries that cost. When energy becomes free or negatively priced, every layer above it becomes easier to move left of zero.

This prediction is not simply that renewable energy will become cheap. Cheap energy still operates on the right side of zero. The threshold that matters is when a country can fund the production, maintenance, and expansion of its energy system while distributing the output to users for free — or with additional value returned. At that point, energy stops being only a commodity. It becomes the foundation of a national abundance stack.

The first country to reach that threshold will not merely lower household bills. It will reduce the cost of industry, transportation, desalination, food production, data centers, robotics, and AI. It will attract people, companies, factories, and capital. And it will pressure every competing country to respond, because the difference will be visible in daily life and in industrial competitiveness.

The framework does not need to predict which country reaches this first. It predicts that the first country to make energy free or negatively priced at scale will gain advantages so large that other countries will be forced to respond — treating energy abundance as a strategic priority, not an environmental preference.

The prediction is falsifiable. If, within twenty-five years, no country distributes energy at zero or negative cost to end users across a double-digit percentage of national consumption, the prediction has failed. If one does, and competing countries respond by treating energy abundance as strategic policy, the prediction holds.

### The Consumer Goods Prediction

The framework predicts that within the next ten to twenty years, free and negatively priced products will become a major, scaled part of how at least one significant beverage operator and at least one significant fast-food operator go to market. Not a pilot. Not a promotion. A core and growing line of the business, funded entirely by non-transactional revenue. The ten-year edge is how fast this can move if a willing operator crosses early; the twenty-year edge is the point by which, if it has not happened, the prediction should be counted as failed.

The two will arrive at it differently, and the difference is part of the prediction.

The beverage mover will most likely not be the largest incumbent. It will be a mid-sized, marketing-native brand — a brand of the Liquid Death type, built recently, comfortable with unconventional models, and unburdened by a century of conventional distribution it would have to defend. Liquid Death is named here as the archetype, not the certainty; if a brand of that profile crosses first, the prediction holds. The giants carry more structural advantages but also more to protect. The first mover will be the brand for which an out-of-the-box revenue model is part of its identity rather than a threat to it.

The large incumbents — Coca-Cola, Pepsi — will move too, but more slowly, and in stages. A company of that size does not leap to free; it walks down the number line. The price falls first, partly offset by non-transactional revenue. Then a product becomes free. Then negatively priced. Each step is validated by the one before it. The demand a company sees when its product is subsidized is the evidence that makes free the rational next move. The demand at free is what makes negative pricing the step after. The crossing is not a single leap requiring nerve. It is a staircase, where each step pays for the next.

What pulls these operators down that staircase is straightforward: there is far more money on the table on the free side than on the paid side. A free or negatively priced cola reaches people the paid product never could — price-sensitive households, lower-income consumers, entire markets where the paid product was a small luxury. The free version does not simply take share from the paid version. It reaches a far larger population, and a far larger population carries a far larger pool of non-transactional revenue with it. The operator that crosses is not sacrificing a business to do a good thing. It is following the money to where there is more of it.

This is why the paid product does not disappear. The prediction is not that these companies become entirely free, but that they run both at once — a free or negatively priced line beside a conventional paid line, the company operating on both sides of zero. The paid line keeps a real and dignified place: there will always be consumers who prefer to simply pay — for nostalgia, for an ad-free product, for the straightforwardness of a transaction. But that segment will be the smaller one. Free Coca-Cola will reach more people than paid Coca-Cola, and over time the free and negatively priced line will be the larger business, because that is where the larger population, and the larger revenue, are.

The fast-food mover will arrive from the opposite direction — from inside a large operator, and from a single location. A free or negatively priced restaurant cannot be switched on across thousands of outlets at once. It has to be proven somewhere first, in one location dense enough and observed enough to establish the model before it scales. The prediction is therefore specific about shape: the first instance will look small — one restaurant, one market — and that small first instance is not a weak version of the prediction but its predicted form. From there it scales, the way any proven model scales.

The prediction is falsifiable. If twenty years pass with no significant beverage operator or no significant fast-food operator having made free or negatively priced products a standing, scaled part of its business through non-transactional revenue — pilots and promotions excluded — the prediction has failed.

### The Digital Advertising to Physical Grocery Prediction

The framework predicts that within the next ten to twenty years, at least one major ad-supported digital platform will become one of the largest grocery operations in the world — offering food and household essentials free or negatively priced to consumers, funded by advertising rather than purchase. The measure is not sales revenue, since the model has no sale price. The measure is the number of households served. The ten-year edge is how fast this can move if the physical layer matures quickly; the twenty-year edge is the point by which, if it has not happened, the prediction should be counted as failed.

The platforms are already most of the way there. Google, Meta, Amazon, TikTok, and their eventual successors already operate the entire advertising engine: targeting, auctions, personalization, advertiser demand, consumer data, and payment infrastructure. What they do not yet have is the physical layer — and that layer is precisely the architecture described earlier in this book: individualized packaging, local production, automated fulfillment, and the consecutive delivery route that carries it to the door. As that layer matures, these companies will be able to attach an advertising engine they have already built to physical goods. Packaging becomes the next advertising interface, and a grocery item is funded the way a video, a search result, or a social feed is funded today — except what the household receives is food, water, and household essentials.

What the physical layer adds is not just reach but a kind of signal the screen could never produce. Everything these platforms currently know about a person is filtered through price and through what competed for their attention in a feed. It is a record of constrained choice. A free or negatively priced essential, chosen and re-chosen by an identified household with price removed from the decision, generates the honest signal this book has described. It is the cleanest read of genuine preference there is — and one their existing business structurally cannot capture. Gathered under the consent the model depends on, that signal is itself part of the incentive to scale. It is not only a larger audience. It is a truer picture of that audience than any screen delivers.

The reason a platform will not merely experiment with this but scale it is the same reason it scaled digital advertising: that is where the larger audience is, and the larger audience carries the larger pool of advertising revenue. A platform that can deliver free groceries reaches a population no paid grocery operation can — and reaches it daily, through a physical good consumed and replaced on a dependable cycle. The incentive does not point toward a pilot. It points toward scale.

The prediction is falsifiable. If twenty years pass with no major ad-supported digital platform offering groceries or household essentials free or negatively priced to consumers — funded by advertising rather than the sale price, as a standing and scaled operation rather than a promotion — the prediction has failed.

### The Ride-Share and Taxi Flip Prediction

The framework predicts that within the next ten to twenty years, an operator of Uber's scale — or a comparable rideshare or robotaxi network — will make free or negatively priced rides its default offering. Not a pilot. Not a single market. The vast majority of its rides across the core of its business. The ten-year edge is how fast this can move if the underlying cost shifts arrive quickly; the twenty-year edge is the point by which, if it has not happened, the prediction should be counted as failed.

The reason is structural. The cost of a ride is a stack of inputs — the driver, the energy, the maintenance, the vehicle itself, and the share of the day the vehicle sits unused — and every one of those inputs is falling. Energy cost per mile is declining. Vehicles are reaching higher range, longer lifespans, and lower production cost. Maintenance is becoming more predictable. Utilization is rising. As each input falls, the remaining cost of a ride shrinks toward a level that non-transactional revenue can cover: advertising, sponsorships, local business partnerships, destination commissions, tourism offers, data services, employer programs, municipal partnerships, and in-vehicle commerce. At that point the ride is no longer only transportation. It becomes a moving platform for media, commerce, data, and distribution — and the fare is no longer the thing that has to pay for it.

The flip does not require every private car to cross. It begins with high-utilization fleets, because a taxi or ride-share vehicle is used far more intensely than a personal one. A privately owned car sits idle most of the day; a mobility fleet can generate value across all of it. That makes fleets the natural first crossing point — the place where the cost stack is thinnest and the non-transactional revenue per vehicle is largest.

The first serious operator to combine a low-cost, high-utilization fleet with a multi-stream revenue architecture will be able to offer free or negatively priced rides. And once it does, competitors will face pressure to match it, for the same reason they always do: users will not choose to pay for a ride when a comparable ride is available at zero or below. The flip, once it begins, propagates — and it propagates toward the default. The prediction is not that a free ride becomes available somewhere. It is that the free ride becomes the *normal* ride: the option most users take, most of the time, across most of an operator's markets.

This is why the paid ride does not disappear. As with every crossing this book describes, the operator ends up running both sides of zero at once. A paid tier remains for the riders who want something the free ride does not offer — a black car, a premium or luxury vehicle, a private ride with no advertising or shared commerce layer, a guaranteed experience. That tier is real, and for the riders who want it, worth paying for. But it is the smaller tier. The free and negatively priced ride is the default and the majority of the business, because that is where the largest population of riders, and the largest pool of non-transactional revenue, are.

The prediction is falsifiable. If twenty years pass with no operator of Uber's scale having made free or negatively priced rides its default offering — the majority of its rides across the core of its markets, not a promotion and not a limited pilot — the prediction has failed.

### Closing

These predictions are offered as the framework's testable claims, organized by domain and timescale to make them scannable against observable reality. The framework predicts structural pressures. The predictions describe what those pressures should produce if they continue to operate as the framework describes. Whether the predictions hold depends on engineering execution, capital allocation, political conditions, and the choices of operators that the framework does not control.

Predictions that miss will be informative, as well as those that hold. A framework that makes specific predictions is a framework that can be tested. Some predictions will be wrong. Some will be partially right in ways that refine the framework's claims. Some will be right in ways that strengthen the framework's standing. All are offered for engagement rather than for acceptance. A single failed prediction does not invalidate every part of the framework — but repeated failures should reduce confidence in its central claims, and this chapter exists to make that accounting unavoidable. The reader who finds errors is invited to trace them back to the underlying framework, not to look away from them.

The architecture below zero is structurally available. The predictions describe what becomes observable if the architecture is built out. Whether it is built out depends on the choices and work of the people who read this book and decide what to do with the framework it offers.

## Chapter 20: The Macroeconomic Objection: A Left-of-Zero Economy in Right-of-Zero Institutions

For thirty years, Japan has been the warning every economist reaches for when someone suggests that falling prices might be good news. Its prices stopped rising in the early 1990s and drifted down or sideways for three decades — the Lost Decades, with their moral: once prices fall, spending stalls, debts grow heavier, and the economy sinks into a torpor it cannot escape. Any framework that drives the cost of essentials toward zero has to answer for Japan first. Because to an economist, that is what this book proposes — a sustained, engineered fall in the price of the things people buy most.

That fall raises two objections that are easy to run together, and only one is on trial here. The first is whether it can be paid for — whether an architecture exists to fund free essentials at scale. The book answers that elsewhere. But the shape of the answer matters here, because a reader who suspects this chapter is dodging it will not trust the rest. The funding does not come from an industry's existing margin — that would be circular, and it would exhaust a finite pool. It comes from value the legacy business never captured: the surplus a participant generates once aggregate efficiency has driven the real cost of the good toward a thin floor, and the new architecture has lifted what can be captured above it. The giveaway lives in the gap between that falling floor and that rising ceiling, and how far below zero a price can go is set by how wide the gap is. FreeWater is the proof that the gap can be positive for a physical good. Whether it stays positive across the whole essentials basket at once is the honest open edge, and it is argued elsewhere, not here. This chapter grants it and turns to the second objection — the one Japan is actually invoked against: when essentials cross zero, what happens to an economy whose debts, wages, taxes, and monetary targets were all written for a world where prices only rise?

The earlier chapters moved one number line and assumed the second would follow. The first is the real economy — the goods and the value they carry, which aggregate efficiency drives left across zero. The second is the nominal economy — the dollars those goods are priced in, and the promises written in them: mortgages, wages, bonds, tax brackets, a central bank's target, each assuming money loses a little value every year. Moving the first while the second stays bolted in place is the objection, and it is right that the two are joined. It is wrong about what travels through the joints. The answer, which folds the objection into the theory rather than patching it, is four claims. The model realigns relative prices rather than collapsing them. The casualties are concentrated, not systemic. The tax base moves rather than vanishes. And the institutions built for rising prices — the two percent target chief among them — are scarcity-era inventions a productivity economy pressures rather than obeys.

### The Objection, at Full Strength

State the objection at its strongest, because the weak version — if everything is free, the economy disappears — is not what a good economist is asking, and Chapter 5 dispatched it: money not spent on water is spent elsewhere, and elsewhere is an economy too. The serious version runs through everything written in fixed dollars, beginning with debt. A loan repays a fixed number of dollars, and its real weight depends on the price level. When prices rise, debts get lighter, and mild inflation quietly transfers value from lender to borrower — which modern credit expects. If prices fall, the calibration inverts: every fixed debt grows heavier in real terms without a clause changing. Irving Fisher named this in 1933 — debt-deflation, the spiral where falling prices raise burdens, which force selling, which pushes prices lower still. It is the most frightening picture in macroeconomics, and it is what the word deflation calls up in a trained mind.

The same problem runs through wages, which are sticky downward — people accept a raise that lags inflation far more readily than a cut to the number on their check. So if the cost of living drops but pay cannot, real labor costs rise, and employers cut jobs instead. It runs through the state, much of whose revenue is a slice of taxable transactions: shift value from priced sales to free goods funded another way, and an economist asks where the tax went. And it runs through the central bank, which targets roughly two percent inflation and has a whole toolkit to defend it. An economy that cheapens its own essentials looks like one fighting its own central bank. That is the objection at full strength — debt, wages, taxes, and monetary policy are all promises written in nominal dollars, and pulling the real economy out from under them strains every joint. The joints are real. What passes through them is far less dangerous than the word that names it.

### Two Kinds of Falling Price

Deflation does enormous dishonest work as a word, because it names two things that share a direction and nothing else. The first is the deflation of collapse: demand falls, and prices fall because no one is buying. Output drops, people lose work, a frightened economy marks its inventory down again and again. That is 1929 to 1933 — catastrophic, because the falling price is the symptom of a system seizing up. The second is the deflation of plenty: prices fall because the real cost of making something dropped — a better process, a cheaper input, a technology that does the same work for less. There, the falling price is not sickness but the visible form of progress. The United States spent the last third of the nineteenth century with gently falling prices and ferocious growth, as mechanization and electrification drove costs down. And when economists examined the full record — most thoroughly Atkeson and Kehoe, across more than a century and many countries — they found the tie between falling prices and depression rests almost entirely on the 1930s, and that most deflations came with no depression at all.

Japan deserves precision, not invocation. Its deflation was mild and persistent, not a spiral, and the stagnation beside it was tangled up with a banking system propped up past resolution, a shrinking and aging workforce, and a decade spent paying down a burst bubble's debts instead of investing. Whether the falling prices caused the stagnation or merely accompanied it is fiercely contested. The honest reading is that Japan shows what a burst bubble does to a fragile banking system in an aging country — mild deflation only one symptom among several. The deflation this framework produces is the second kind: aggregate efficiency lowers the real cost of producing essentials, so water, energy, freight, and food get cheaper because making and moving them genuinely costs less, not because demand collapsed. A model built on rising efficiency does not, by itself, produce the deflation of collapse — that takes nominal demand contracting on top of it. Even a clean transition still strands the specific contracts it crosses, which a later section takes up. But the objection borrows its terror from 1929, and the mechanism is closer to 1880.

One confusion sits underneath the whole Japan anxiety. The "negative" in a negatively priced good is not the "negative" of a negative interest rate. They are nearly opposite things wearing the same word. A negative interest rate is desperation — a central bank shoving money out the door to force spending in an economy so starved of demand that people would rather hold cash and watch prices fall. It signals weakness. A negative price is the reverse: a business choosing to pay the person who takes its product, because the architecture around it — sponsorship, efficiency, the value the participant generates — throws off enough surplus to fund the gift. One is the economy failing. The other is it working.

### Relative Prices, Not a Falling Price Level

The deeper answer is not just that this is the good kind of deflation. Across the whole economy, it may not be aggregate deflation at all — it is a realignment of relative prices, and that distinction is decisive for the part of the objection that frightens economists most. Recall the Discretionary Expansion Effect from Chapter 5: when essentials get cheaper, the money households used to spend on them does not evaporate. It moves toward what they could not previously afford. Essentials fall because their real cost fell. Discretionary prices face upward pressure because freed purchasing power is now chasing them. These are two sides of one transfer of spending, and the overall price level is a weighted average of both — an average in which one set falls while another rises need not fall at all.

Fisher's spiral requires a falling general price level. It is the average that inflates debt, not the price of any one good. A mortgage does not get heavier because water got cheaper — only if the whole price level falls, dragging nominal incomes down while the fixed debt holds. The thing that would set off the bomb is precisely the thing the model does not generate.

Whether the net effect even shows up as falling prices is, in the end, a monetary choice — and this is where the GDP question resolves. Real output clearly rises, because the point is to make more for less and reach people the paid economy never served. The nominal figure — GDP in dollars — is whatever the central bank's stance makes it. Hold total spending stable while those dollars buy more, and falling and rising prices net near zero: nominal incomes intact as real incomes climb. Wages move the same way. In a productivity boom, nominal pay need not fall — it can hold or rise while prices drop, and a steady paycheck against a falling cost of living is a real raise. Across the economy as a whole, pay is only cut in the collapse kind of deflation. The exception is the sector the crossing is passing through — and that exception gets its own section below. So the household outside the crossing's path gains twice — cheaper essentials and intact wages — and its fixed debts get easier to carry, not heavier. The danger arrives only if policy lets total spending contract.

That "only if" deserves more weight than a clause, because it carries the benign verdict. Holding total spending stable while prices fall is something a central bank must do deliberately — and not for a quarter, but across the decades a structural transition takes. The record is mixed. The early 1930s were a monetary collapse the Federal Reserve failed to arrest. The years from 2008 to 2015 were, by a common reading, a failure to accommodate quickly enough. The Bank of Japan spent the 1990s arguably too tight for too long. None of these had abundance as a cause, but each shows the same institution being asked to do the one thing this framework leans on, and not always doing it. So the honest headline is conditional: with competent monetary policy, an economy of falling real costs need not become one of falling nominal incomes. The realignment is real. Whether it nets to stable prices rather than a contraction is a monetary achievement, not an automatic one. The structural case removes the reason deflation would happen — not the institution that could let it happen anyway.

### A Floor Without a Transfer

What abundance does for people has a familiar shape with an unfamiliar engine. Tally what a household stops paying as essentials cross zero — groceries, transport, energy, water, connectivity — and the value freed up dwarfs any check a government was realistically going to fund. Abundance functionally resembles a large and growing cost-of-living dividend, delivered to everyone, at a scale no transfer program would reach. And the engine is the opposite of a basic income's. A basic income is redistribution — dollars taxed from some and handed to others, a transfer that must be funded and drags every standard objection behind it: cost, work incentives, and above all inflation, since handing out cash adds dollars chasing the same goods. This is not a transfer. No one is taxed to fund it, and the dividend comes from efficiency and sponsorship inside the system. It delivers that floor while pushing prices down — more goods, not more dollars — exactly where a cash basic income pushes them up. The claim is directional, not a literal sum: a free good is worth its price to the holder, not a pile of redirectable cash.

That same engine answers the tax question, because funding and taxation are one mechanism seen from two sides. The fear is that free goods erase the sales the state used to tax. The clearest evidence already runs at planetary scale — and it settles the tax question even if, on its own, it does not prove a physical essential can be funded this way, which is the funding question, not this one. Google and Meta give their products to billions and throw off enormous taxable profit. The ad-supported model does not shrink the base — it relocates it, from the cash register to corporate income. And we know that income is taxable, because governments have raced to levy digital-services taxes aimed at ad revenue. You do not write a new tax for something that is not there. Nor does the point depend on advertising. Where the funding is sponsorship and efficiency, those payments and that surplus are taxable revenue all the same — and the money households save flows into discretionary spending that is taxed too, often more heavily than the essentials it replaced. There is always a taxable event. It simply is not at the old checkout counter.

What survives of the worry is not a vanishing base but a question of who collects — and conceding it is what makes the rest credible. Sales tax and corporate income tax are different buckets. In the United States, sales tax funds states and school districts, while corporate tax flows mostly to the federal level. The aggregate base holds, but a town funded by grocery sales tax can lose its slice even as the national government gains. That is a real transition problem and a solvable one — a code written for where value used to sit can be rewritten to follow it. Against a basic income, the contrast on the public ledger is total: a cash dividend costs the government a transfer it must fund, while abundance generates taxable activity and delivers the same floor.

### The Stranded Contract

None of this makes the crossing painless, and the chapter would lose credibility pretending otherwise. Realignment is benign in the aggregate and brutal in the particular. Step into a single industry as the crossing reaches it, and the monetary problem is not dissolved but concentrated. A firm, a worker, an owner inside an essential sector is about to watch revenue fall — that is what it means for the thing they sell to move toward zero — but their debts do not fall with it. The loan on the equipment, the mortgage on the warehouse, the financing on the truck were written in fixed dollars against fixed revenue. They stay the size they were while the revenue meant to service them collapses. That is debt-deflation, concentrated onto the balance sheets the crossing passes through, rather than spread thin across an economy where realignment defuses it. For them, locally, the spiral is real.

Chapter 13 told this story as a labor story. The eighty-four thousand Blockbuster jobs that ended. The New York taxi medallions that traded near a million dollars at their 2013 peak and collapsed within a few years of ride-share — described there as the transition's human cost, falling hardest on those least able to absorb it. But a medallion is not only a job; it is a financial instrument. Thousands were bought on credit as a retirement asset — a nominal loan against a value everyone assumed would hold. When the value cratered, the loan did not. Owners were left servicing a six-figure debt against an asset worth a fraction of it — in the worst cases, as Chapter 13 noted, contributing to documented suicides. That is the stranded contract at its starkest: the monetary twin of the labor displacement, the same casualty in a different ledger.

So the honest macroeconomic risk is not the economy-wide spiral the objection fears. It is the stranded contract — concentrated, sectoral debt-deflation where the crossing lands, even as the broad price level holds. That is smaller and more precise, and precision is what makes it addressable. A general spiral would be beyond reach; a stranded contract is local and identifiable — and it stays bounded so long as the losses are not concentrated on the balance sheets that multiply them. Enough correlated contracts stranding at once, piled into the same banks, pensions, and municipal budgets, is how a sectoral loss has historically gone systemic. Keeping the crossing's losses diversified and paced is part of the transition's design, not a footnote to it. And it arrives, unlike the 1930s, in an economy generating surplus as it crosses — surplus a well-designed transition can turn toward the balance sheets it breaks, through forbearance, transition financing, deliberate cushioning. Whether that is done is a question of design and will, as Chapter 13 said of the displaced workers. The framework cannot promise it. It can say the resource exists — which the transitions that stranded the medallion owners did not have.

### The Two Percent Target Is an Artifact

That leaves the central bank, and here the framework does not so much accommodate the two percent target as predict pressure on it. The target is not a law of nature. It is a recent invention, adopted across the developed world only in the last few decades, to solve problems a left-of-zero economy reshapes. Ask why two percent and not zero, and the answer is made almost entirely of frictions already named. A positive buffer gives real interest rates room to go negative in a downturn without nominal rates having to. It greases sticky wages, letting real pay fall through inflation so nominal pay never has to be cut. And it holds a safe distance from the deflation of collapse. Each is a scarcity-era problem a productivity economy meets differently: the collapse it fears is the deflation realignment does not produce, and the wage problem softens when real incomes rise through falling costs. The buffer stays useful, so the target does not vanish. But the case for two specifically, rather than lower, weakens as real costs trend down on their own.

And there is a coherent, long-standing alternative — not a fringe one. The productivity norm, set out in modern form by George Selgin in a 1997 book whose title is the thesis — the case for a falling price level in a growing economy — holds that when productivity rises, the healthy behavior of the price level is to drift gently down, handing efficiency's gains to everyone holding money. An economy straining to manufacture two percent on top of real gains is suppressing a signal, not stabilizing one. Its contemporary cousin targets the total flow of nominal spending rather than the inflation rate. The book need not settle that debate. The two percent target is one institutional choice among several, built for rising prices — and a framework that drives real costs structurally down exposes it as a variable under pressure as more of the economy crosses. That pressure is not a flaw in the framework. It is one of its predictions. Further out, the same logic points past what a central bank targets toward what a central bank is — but that belongs with the governance chapter, and it is the speculative end of this one.

### What This Costs, and What It Doesn't

Having run through debt, prices, taxes, and the central bank, the chapter owes a plain statement of where it ends. What the framework does not produce is the catastrophe the objection summons — a general fall in the price level, a Fisherian spiral, an economy talking itself into torpor. Its falling prices are the deflation of plenty, not collapse, and what it spreads across the basket is relative-price realignment, not an aggregate fall. What it does produce is real but concentrated and bounded: stranded contracts where the crossing lands, and nominal institutions slow to be redrawn for it.

Stated as one claim, the verdict is conditional twice over, and both conditions have already been named. Given a funding gap that stays positive across the basket, and a central bank competent enough to hold nominal spending stable through the transition, the crossing realigns relative prices rather than collapsing them — stranding specific contracts, but not the system. Neither condition is automatic, and the chapter has tried to mark each one honestly rather than wave it through: the funding gap up front, the monetary one at the end. What is worth seeing is that the two halves of the argument are the same move performed twice. The funding objection says a finite slice of revenue cannot carry the whole essentials economy; the answer is that the giveaway lives in the gap between a falling cost floor and a rising capture ceiling. The monetary objection says falling essential prices must drag the whole price level down; the answer is that the realignment lives in the gap between falling essentials and rising discretionary prices. Both take an apparent aggregate catastrophe and resolve it into a bounded, local one — a slice mistaken for the system, a sector mistaken for the economy. The same distinction that funds the crossing is the one that keeps it from collapsing.

Money does not vanish here. Institutions this embedded are sticky, which is why they adapt at the pace of redesign rather than collapsing. The mismatch between a left-of-zero real economy and right-of-zero nominal institutions is the genuine risk, and it is one of design and will, not of mathematics: the mathematics says the crossing happens, not that it happens gently. The danger is smaller, more local, and more reachable than the word that names it makes it sound.

## A Message from the Author

A book like this is best understood as an argument, not a conclusion — and I want to be direct about how I hope it is read.

For a decade, the economics of free and negatively priced systems has been the central focus of my working life. I have run live experiments across many categories and countries, and I have published the results — including the full operational manual that preceded this book — in the open, for anyone to use, copy, or improve. I mention this not as a credential but as context for what follows. The framework in these pages did not come from theory. It came from a decade of engineering and watching what held.

That same decade is why I am certain the work is not finished. A framework of this scope — spanning economics, logistics, energy, advertising, manufacturing, and data — has more surface area than any one person can fully verify alone, however long they have spent on it. It improves by being examined. And I have spent years inviting exactly that.

So, I have done the obvious thing. I have put these ideas in front of anyone willing to challenge them — economists, operators, engineers, investors, skeptics of every kind — and asked them to find what is wrong. That invitation has been open a long time, and I want to be plain about the result. I do not claim the work is finished or beyond correction; a claim like that would contradict the spirit of this page. What I can report is simply what has happened: the objections have been raised seriously, by capable people, and so far, each one has either been answered or has made the framework better. That record — not certainty — is why I am confident enough to keep the invitation open.

And that invitation is the point of this page. If you find something here you believe is mistaken — a mechanism that does not hold, an incentive I have misjudged, a constraint I have not properly accounted for — I want to hear it, directly and specifically. A clearly identified flaw is not a problem for this project; it is the most useful thing a reader can offer it. I would rather correct an error than defend one, and when an objection is real, I work it quickly. If you see one, bring it.

If what you find instead is a part of this you would want to develop further, I am equally interested. Much of what these books describe deserves rigorous, formal treatment of a kind a single author working across every field at once cannot give it. If you are a researcher or practitioner whose discipline is touched by something in these pages, and you see a piece of it worth taking further, I would welcome the chance to co-author that work. A single author reasoning across many disciplines can sketch the map. Specialists are what turn a sketch into something rigorous and complete.

This book, and the one before it, are offered in that spirit: a serious argument, put forward in good faith, built to be tested. It represents where a decade of work has reached — not where it ends. The framework here is the foundation for what comes next, not the last word on it. **It has been tested, it has held up so far, and the invitation stands: if you would like to challenge it, strengthen it, or build on it, I would be glad to hear from you.**

— Josh Cliffords

## Acknowledgments

My deepest gratitude goes to my wife, Ivana Cliffords, who has been beside me from the beginning. She believed in these ideas long before they were fully formed, and her careful editing and sharp eye shaped the book they became.

I owe an equal debt to my late mother, Gloria. She was the first person to invest in my work — \$25,000 that became the seed capital for FreeWater — but her greater contribution was a piece of advice. When I first began developing negatively priced concepts like FreeTaxi and early regenerative energy systems, she told me that starting with ideas that large would mean failing before anyone could understand them. Begin instead, she said, with something simple enough that an ordinary person could build it — a better-than-free beverage, a shirt, a small food product — so the world could see the model working in a real example before seeing how far it might be taken. She was right, and that insight is the foundation everything here is built on.

To everyone who has contributed to the FreeWater project and helped experiment with free and negatively priced systems: thank you for your persistence and your willingness to try something that once sounded impossible.

To our community of more than 1.4 million subscribers around the world — thank you for sharing the idea, offering feedback, donating, and helping demonstrate that generosity can scale.

To the entrepreneurs across dozens of countries who have launched their own free and negatively priced projects: you have shown that this work belongs to everyone, not to any one of us.

To everyone who read *How to Make a Free Product Company*, whether to support the work or to build your own version of it — your willingness to experiment carried these ideas further than I could have alone.

And to the manufacturers and partners who took early risks to bring FreeWater into the world: thank you for betting on it before it was proven.

The first version of this was yours to make possible. The next is everyone's to build.
